#200. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

#197. Trump wanted Jensen Huang back. Now, the president is all alone in Washington

President Donald Trump wanted Jensen Huang back at the helm of Nvidia. Now that the chip mogul is reinstated, Trump is cheering alone in Washington.

Trump celebrated and claimed credit after Huang was reinstated by the Nvidia board on Monday, a stunning return to power for the embattled CEO. In a nearly hourlong phone call with CNBC, the president went as far as saying he "made the deal" to bring Huang back.

But Trump's jubilation stands in stark contrast to the reaction from even his staunchest allies on Capitol Hill, who recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang — whose relationship with a potential Chinese intelligence asset they fear may have jeopardized U.S. national security.

"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States."

Trump's rare isolation from his party over Huang comes after the GOP was throttled in the midterm elections, as Democrats rode a blue tsunami to capture the Senate and the largest House majority this century. And it suggests Republicans, who have spent a decade in lockstep with the president, may have found an issue on which they can't bring themselves to take Trump's side.

"I can accept what the president has done," Cramer said. "But we have to put guardrails around to protect the United States of America."

Many midterm voters identified Silicon Valley's most visible outputs, artificial intelligence and data centers, as top concerns motivating them to cast ballots for Democrats. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey in October found 63% of registered voters oppose an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% support one.

By timing his extraordinary intervention in Nvidia so soon after the GOP's historic losses, Trump could spark a national debate about corporate governance and responsibility, former Under Secretary of State Richard Stengel said.

"With the repudiation of Trumpism in the midterms and voter revulsion with corruption, I think there will be a turn back to a more value-based duty of care for boards of directors and away from the current maximization of shareholder value," Stengel told CNBC in an email.

"Directors are citizens first, shareholders second. The idea that you put profit above national security should disgust shareholders, not please them," Stengel, who worked in the Obama administration, wrote.

Bipartisan lawmakers are now racing to pass a series of export control bills before the year ends, a call that has only grown louder since Huang was reinstated.

There's "not just frustration or political worry, but actual, substantive national security concern here from Democrats and Republicans," Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, told CNBC.

"This has historically been a bipartisan issue," McGuire said. "Democrats and Republicans generally pretty much agree that we shouldn't be sending our most advanced chips to China."

"If it happened in massive quantities, people want to know why, how, and they want to make sure that it doesn't happen again," he said. "And putting your thumb on the scale in this clear of a way seems like it could undercut those efforts."

The White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on the political dynamics of Trump's involvement in reinstating Huang.

Republicans will have the first crack at investigating Huang next week before ceding power to Democrats in January. Democrats have vowed their own investigations into the Nvidia CEO and his ties to the president and his Cabinet. The Democrats who will lead those investigations are now hopeful that Republicans will join them.

"I've been seeing a lot of Republicans outraged and scandalized by what has just happened," House Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin, D-Md., said Tuesday on "Squawk Box," when asked if Republicans may support a bipartisan investigation of Nvidia.

"When else in American history can you think of a time when a president of the United States called up a corporate board of directors meeting and then essentially dictated to them who was going to be their new CEO right after they had fired the guy?"

Trump throughout his second term has stated repeatedly that achieving AI dominance is required for the U.S. to maintain global superiority. His administration has championed the rapid growth of the country's tech giants and forged close ties with its leaders, especially Huang, while removing constraints on the industry and opposing some new regulatory efforts.

At the same time, Trump has defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick against accusations of slow-walking export controls on China, the fiercest competitor in the AI race. And while Trump recently discussed AI during an in-person meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, he appeared to deliver few demands and achieved few assurances about Beijing's efforts to acquire U.S. chips.

"To say we can't do AI safety because of China, and then also be actively loosening the controls on China, makes no sense," McGuire said.

Some of Trump's fiercest allies have raised similar qualms.

"Reinstating Jensen Huang is going to blow back on our national security," MAGA activist Laura Loomer wrote in an X post Monday. "Eventually there has to be accountability for jeopardizing US national security. China didn't just get access to Nvidia chips by accident."

Newt Gingrich, the influential former Republican speaker of the House, in an X post after Huang's reinstatement, wrote, "Congress should really dig into this as a national security issue of enormous proportions and also a significant failure in the federal bureaucracy."

Their criticisms sharply contrast with the praise coming from Silicon Valley and some of the Trump-friendly voices within it, such as Elon Musk, who wrote Monday, "The mistake has been corrected."

But that's a shrinking political constituency, McGuire said.

"The tech-right perspective is very out of touch with where most of the Republican Party is on these issues, to say nothing of the Democratic Party," he said.

"Those positions are just not going to be sustainable for much longer," he added. "I think they're already becoming unsustainable."

#196. Walmart hikes outlook as CEO tells CNBC retailer aims to keep prices low despite soaring fuel costs

Walmart on Thursday reported earnings and revenue that narrowly topped estimates and raised its full-year outlook as it saw strong e-commerce growth ahead of the busy holiday season.

Walmart said global e-commerce sales surged 17% in the fiscal third quarter, driven by store-fulfilled pickup and delivery, with Walmart U.S. up 23% and Sam's Club U.S. rising 30%. Its Walmart U.S. advertising business saw a 37% sales increase for the quarter.

Walmart said it rolled back prices on nearly 8,000 products across its stores, a focus as U.S. consumers worry about the cost of living in the face of elevated food and gas prices.

CEO John Furner told CNBC's Becky Quick that the company has seen higher-than-expected diesel costs, but that tariff refunds have helped the retailer to offset those inputs and maintain lower prices.

"The reality is that increases in diesel fuel and transportation, they do work their way into cost of goods," Furner said in an exclusive interview. "So we're hoping to see these costs come down as soon as possible, and we'll keep prices as low as we can."

CFO John David Rainey told CNBC that growth in online orders for Walmart and Sam's Club was driven by an emphasis on speed of delivery and reduced prices. He added that Walmart saw its largest share gains in the food category in three years during the third quarter.

"This was another really solid quarter for us, we feel good about it," Rainey told CNBC. "We continue to gain share while continuing to improve the profitability of our digital businesses."

Walmart also raised its full-year guidance, now expecting net sales to increase between 4.9% and 5.2%, compared with previous guidance of a 4% to 5% increase. Rainey said Walmart is maintaining "flexibility" in its guidance.

Walmart anticipates adjusted earnings per share will come in between $2.84 and $2.89, compared with a prior range of $2.80 to $2.87. It's also now expecting adjusted operating income to increase between 8% and 8.6%, up from the previous outlook of between 7% and 8.5% growth.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, the company reported net income of $4.95 billion, or 62 cents per share, down from $6.14 billion, or 77 cents per share, a year earlier. Revenue rose 5.2%.

Adjusting for investment gains and losses, tax matters and reorganization charges, Walmart earned 65 cents per share.

Walmart U.S. recorded net sales of $125 billion, up 3.6% from a year earlier. Its comparable sales excluding fuel rose 2.9%, in line with Wall Street expectations, according to StreetAccount.

Furner also said Walmart is seeing encouraging adoption of its artificial intelligence agent, named Sparky, especially ahead of the holiday season. He said it's led to customer basket increases of 40% on average.

"It just is a great way of just completing the entire basket and solving entire the solution for people," he said.

Sam's Club reported net sales of $25.9 billion, marking a 10% increase from the year-ago period. It said its sales were driven by strength in the grocery category and general merchandise.

In October, Walmart announced it was launching a joint membership option with Walmart+ and Sam's Club as it leans further into convenience. Both stores will still offer stand-alone memberships, as well.

Furner told CNBC the new membership has brought roughly 2 million new people into Sam's Club since its launch in late October. Though the company reimbursed some members for the new membership rate, Rainey added that it's already seeing positive returns on the joint model and that consumers are "spending appreciably more."

The company on Thursday said its global membership fee revenue grew 17% in the third quarter.

The company is typically well insulated from macroeconomic pressures because of its value reputation. Last quarter, the company said it was lowering prices of products across multiple categories, including beef.

Rainey said Thursday that he's "optimistic" about consumer spending heading into the holiday season and that the fourth quarter is already "off to a pretty good start."

The big-box retailer has posted solid growth over the past few quarters as it sees customers gravitate to reduced prices, improvements in delivery and gains in its advertising business. It's also leaned into attracting higher-income consumers with its Walmart+ membership.

Shares of Walmart have fluctuated this year but are up roughly 13% year to date.

#195. San Francisco is enjoying an AI boom, but worries about a crash are growing

The city of San Francisco is the heart of the AI sector

Lauren Goldboss is busy. The owner of a small San Francisco dog-walking company, she has been hiring staff and buying vans.

Fuelling the growth is her new office dog-walking service Paw Perk, popular with some local AI start-ups.

Her business is an example of how the AI boom in San Francisco is benefiting other sectors.

The city saw an exodus of residents during the pandemic and its downtown was weakened by remote work, but it's now enjoying a rejuvenation, with AI being the major driver.

Lauren Goldboss walks dogs for AI workers

Leading AI firms OpenAI and Anthropic â which have been hiring top engineering talent, often on eye-popping salaries â are based here. Anthropic, the company behind the Claude chatbot, last week listed on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

Thousands of other AI start-ups have also sprung up, external in San Francisco.

Signs of the boom are evident in soaring rents and house prices, falling office vacancies, and buses and billboards plastered with cryptic AI adverts.

But what if there is a significant downward correction in AI share prices and a resulting big fall in investment in the industry? The International Monetary Fund last month became the latest organization to warn, external that this was a possibility.

Concerns have since continued due to the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang, the former boss of AI firm Nvidia, based south of San Francisco, causing some wider AI stock falls. However, Nvidia remains highly profitable, and Wednesday of this week it reported that its latest quarterly profits had doubled from a year earlier to $65bn (Â£49bn).

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Wealthy AI workers send San Francisco house prices soaring

Follow the money: Who pays for the AI boom now?

What are the thoughts of AI workers in the city, and should San Francisco â no stranger to tech's boom-and-bust cycles â be preparing for a possible crash?

One AI worker employed in the city is convinced that a crash is coming. "I am certain that the house of cards will come downâ¦ and I'm sure it will affect me," says the man who asked that we didn't use his name.

He questions whether leading US AI companies can generate enough revenues to justify their enormous spending commitments as cheaper, increasingly capable AI models emerge, especially from China.

But he isn't losing sleep over it. "Why worry about things I can't control?"

Another San Francisco-based AI worker, who also asked to remain anonymous, says that a significant market correction is "totally possible".

Yet he adds that most people don't think of it as the core risk. Instead, he says that the bigger concern in his circles, and the one he shares, is the existential risk that AI could pose to humanity.

"In some weird way, a market correction being the worst-case outcome feels great."

If there is an AI crash, San Francisco could be hit far more than Silicon Valley which lies 40 miles to its south.

That's because the AI boom is very much centred on the city itself rather than the wider San Francisco Bay Area, which includes Silicon Valley.

One study, external found that 60% of all venture capital invested in US AI firms from 2023 to 2025 went to the companies located in San Francisco, dwarfing the significantly smaller percentage given to companies in Silicon Valley.

But some factors suggest that the impact of a crash in San Francisco may be softer than some fear.

For all the investment they have attracted, AI companies are employing relatively few workers in the city compared to previous tech booms. So there may not be that many jobs to lose in a downturn.

"Hiring isn't necessarily on the scale as we would have thought with the amount of venture capital and new company formation," says Jeff Bellisario, executive director of the Bay Area Council Economic Institute, a business-affiliated think tank.

Ted Egan, the chief economist for the City and County of San Francisco, adds that "because of AI, it is a good time for tech again in the city, but we are not seeing tonnes of overall tech job growth".

Despite the expansion of the AI sector, the city remains about 35,000 jobs shy of its pre-pandemic peak, estimates Enrico Moretti, an economics professor at the University of California, Berkeley.

Instead, much of the spending by AI firms in the US is going towards data centres and computing power, relatively little of which are located in the Bay Area. San Francisco might be the brains of the AI boom, but it isn't the brawn.

Nonetheless, a significant correction could still be "very painful" for San Francisco, warns Moretti. Less AI hiring and potential layoffs would ripple through the local service sector, likely to affect jobs in everything from restaurants, to retail, to personal services like dog walking.

"It is the spillover on service jobs that would actually be the main effect," he says.

Office vacancies could also rise, reducing downtown foot traffic, while rents and house prices could weaken.

Huge data centres are being built across the US to power AI

But AI isn't the only force driving San Francisco's recovery. Healthcare and tourism â which the city has been working to encourage, including by improving downtown safety and cleanliness â are also growing,, external which may help cushion against a possible AI downturn.

"Tourism is not fully back, but the growth has been healthy," says Egan, adding that it is a sign of confidence in San Francisco.

All told, Egan doesn't expect an AI crash to fundamentally alter San Francisco's economic trajectory.

"The city's economy has not been screaming during this AI boom, so I wouldn't expect it to grind to a halt if there was a slowdown in AI spending," he says. "I think of it primarily as a hiccup that we should expect, rather than a major course correction for the city's economy."

As to whether and how San Francisco's city government should prepare, there are limits to what it can do, note Bellisario and Moretti. But encouraging growth in sectors beyond technology and maintaining financial reserves could help.

For Goldboss, the dog-walking company owner, her philosophy is to "build while it is here". If the next tech bust comes â "always a reality in San Francisco" â she will try to adapt to what the market needs. "It is just the risk you kind of have to take," she says.

#192. Gap names new Athleta CEO, says Old Navy turnaround is seeing signs of progress

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 11% Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."

#191. I pay £250 a month for someone else to do my life admin - it's worth it

Maxine Dillon says she needed help to get through her to-do list

Maxine Dillon, a busy 44-year-old mum of two, was struggling with her life admin â the never-ending to-do list of household and personal chores.

Everything from arranging for a handyman to fix something, to posting her clothing returns on Vinted, she wasn't getting around to it.

"I felt like all the little jobs that I didn't have time for were mounting up," says Maxine, who is self-employed helping businesses with their use of social media.

Then last year, Maxine, who lives in Mansfield in Nottinghamshire, discovered a new company called Life Laundry.

Launched in 2025 by two women based in Wakefield, Yorkshire, you can pay them to do all your admin jobs for you. Such companies are known as life personal assistants, and in recent years a growing number have sprung up on both sides of the Atlantic.

Maxine, who earns Â£32,000 a year, doesn't have a massive amount of disposable income, but she decided to start spending Â£250 a month for five hours' work and thinks it is worth it as it also helps reduce her "mental load".

The owners of Life Laundry, Alicia Papachristou and Jane Forbes, now look after a host of things for Maxine. Other jobs they take care of include arranging insurance, sending school emails, and managing online subscriptions.

Much of the work they can do remotely, but once a month they travel to Mansfield to do the jobs that require them to be physically present.

"If someone asks us what we do, we say we're life PAs [personal assistants], and we ease your overwhelm," says Forbes.

Amanda says she and her husband made a conscious decision to spend more quality time with their family

With such companies also increasingly available in the US, another busy working mother, Amanda Sims Nichols in Austin, Texas, has also employed a firm to look after her life admin.

Amanda, who is a business consultant, has three children with her attorney husband, and decided to start paying for help with life admin after moving house, away from her family support network.

She pays $2,250 (Â£1,750) a month for 25 hours, and says she relishes offloading regular chores including the weekly food shop and clothes washing.

"Money cannot buy you everything, but it can buy you back some of your time," says the 45-year-old.

The company she uses, Queen of To Do, was founded by Kate Ginsberg in 2011. She says that interest has greatly increased since the pandemic.

The business now has more than 50 active users. Meanwhile, Life Laundry has 25 customers.

Both companies say demand is growing for their services as people's lives become increasingly frantic.

Ginsberg says that the work they do for clients can be very diverse, with no task too big or too small.

Some of the larger tasks her team has faced over the years include flying from Austin to New York City to buy a desperately needed Christmas gift, and finding a new wedding location for a client with just three days' notice due to an incoming hurricane.

Staff of life admin companies will do most jobs around the house for a client

Jess Baker, a UK chartered psychologist, says the appeal of such providers is understandable.

"Using a life PA, if you can afford one, takes the pressure off having to keep hundreds of to-dos swimming around your head, or on lists you might forget to look at," she says.

"Otherwise, the cognitive load can lead to feelings of being overwhelmed, excess worry and exhaustion."

While paying for help with such tasks may seem a luxury, for some people support with life's everyday trials is a lifeline.

Anna Allington sought help after her husband died. "My husband passed away. I was 47," she says.

"And the same week he passed away, I got leukaemia. There was a lot to sort out and I was very, very unwell."

Anna, now 51, still suffers the after-effects of her cancer. "I can't function executively like I used to," she says.

She pays Â£1,200 a month for 40 hours of help from Cheshire-based company Vi-Va. Her personal assistant helps her respond to emails, manage her diary and make travel plans and bookings.

"We meet twice a week on Zoom," says Anna, who lives in the Cotswolds, so they can go through all her tasks.

Many of us would love for someone else to do our laundry

Many more women than men use these companies.

Queen of To Do says just 20% of its clients are male, while only one of Life Laundry's 25 clients is a man.

Some of that disparity is likely due to mothers still looking after the lion's share of household admin, as studies continue to show.

However, Life Laundry's Forbes wonders if some men are too proud to ask for assistance.

"Men are struggling as well, but I think they're less likely to ask for help."

#190. Crypto talent will return ‘when AI implodes,’ Arthur Hayes says

Startup talent that has concentrated in artificial intelligence will return to the crypto industry "when AI implodes," according to Arthur Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said in an online interview with CNBC on Tuesday.

He added that some founders may also return once they "recognize" that their products could eventually be "usurped by Anthropic or OpenAI."

The scale and capabilities of the largest frontier AI labs have made it difficult to "build a real AI business," Hayes said, arguing that "most" founders simply "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to face "serious headwinds" in the second half of 2027, when leading labs begin confronting sizable bills tied to compute leases and other non-cancelable commitments.

Anthropic expects about $451.6 billion in compute and infrastructure obligations through the end of 2030, with roughly $70.7 billion due by the end of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Broader enthusiasm around the AI trade has also cooled amid questions about future infrastructure spending. Even exuberance from Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO on Monday proved short-lived, with the shares falling 5.9% on Tuesday and Anthropic dropping below its IPO price four days after its debut.

The co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX also thinks bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to fall. The cryptocurrency has shown little sign of recovery after dropping as low as $55,142.16 on Saturday — its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin hit an all-time high of $126,279.63 in October last year and traded as high as $97,915.90 in January this year.

It has now fallen for seven straight weeks — only the third such streak in its history — with crypto exchange Bitfinex attributing the weakness to equity-market sentiment rather than interest-rate expectations.

Hayes said he would like to see bitcoin rebound from around $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to fall. That divergence, he said, would suggest markets were shifting from a broad sell-off toward pricing in a "reflation" scenario, with the Federal Reserve eventually providing more liquidity.

Neither bitcoin nor gold has yet decoupled from the Nasdaq, with Hayes describing the current market moves as a "correlation-led liquidation."

"Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said.

Hayes said a bitcoin rally could push up funding rates and futures-basis returns, increasing the income Ethena earns from its hedged crypto positions. That could make USDe, Ethena's synthetic dollar, more attractive and expand its circulation, he said, adding that he expects the ENA token to rise materially.

"Those are the two things that I'm keeping an eye on, and when I'm ready to pull the trigger on buying risk again, those will be the things we buy a lot of," Hayes said.

#187. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.

#186. How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Nvidia and its co-founder Jensen Huang have been at the centre of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom.

No company or chief executive has arguably been more consequential to the development of AI in recent years, with the US microchip manufacturer under Huang positioning itself as a critical cog in the development of the world-changing technology.

That central position prompted questions, in the aftermath of Huang's departure over revelations of extramarital affairs, over how important he was to the wider AI industry and US economy.

The fact he has been handed his job back after just 11 days in a U-turn by Nvidia's board implies the top brass believe Huang is irreplaceable.

Shares in the company have surged on the news of Huang's shock return, following a period of sharp declines.

While Nvidia is not as much of a household name as other Big Tech players such as Apple, Amazon and Microsoft, the company has been just as integral to the booming US stock market of recent years.

The billions of dollars being funnelled into AI and the construction of data centres has bolstered the American economy, with Nvidia being one of the biggest spenders.

Originally focusing on chips which process graphics for computer games, it pivoted to making the chips used in AI systems and now dominates the market.

It is now one the most valuable firms in the world and its rapid growth is poised to continue as the firm's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier.

Huang played a major role in the company, leading its shift towards AI, and maintained a firm grip on it, with up to 200 people directly reporting to him.

Some suggested his departure, which was sparked by the emergence that Huang had an affair with a woman alleged to have been a spy for China, posed a risk to the wider AI industry.

That was because it was unclear whether Nvidia would change strategy and potentially rein in spending - a move could have had wider implications for the US economy, but which looks unlikely now Huang has returned.

The fears were sparked due to Nvidia being a key source of finance for many firms, as well as being a microchip supplier. On its journey to the top of the AI industry, it has, through Huang, created a spider's web of deals through what is known as "circular financing".

Not only does it sell microchips to its customers, which include the likes of Meta, SpaceX and OpenAI, it has also provided funds for them and other firms in the supply chain by either handing out loans or investing in them directly.

The practice has prompted concerns among some investors, who worry what might happen if Nvidia were to turn off the spending taps. If investment dried up, would other firms in the supply chain cut spending or jobs themselves? Would that spread to the wider US economy?

That risk was perhaps why tech bosses including Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, the CEO of Fortnite maker Epic Games, had called for Huang's reinstatement.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation, and backed a campaign, along with shareholders and supporters, to "Bring Jensen Back".

Following the announcement of Huang's return, Musk posted: "The mistake has been corrected."

Kathleen Brooks, research director at investment platform XTB, said Huang's return suggested he was "the godfather" of AI and has the status to shift "the dial for corporate finance".

"Is Jensen Huang the most valuable man on the planet? His return suggests more of the same when it comes to circular financing and the rapid AI infrastructure build out, and the market loves it," she added.

However, Huang's return to Nvidia does not solve the wider issues rattling the tech stock markets in recent weeks.

Investors had taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will actually pay off.

However, investment in AI is still expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, who said the technology would remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

The value of the shares in Big Tech companies have cushioned the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, buoying investments and pensions for people worldwide.

President Donald Trump hailed Huang as a "great patriot and national treasure" after his reappointment and pointed to the chief executive's wider impact.

"The country needs Jensen's leadership at Nvidia in order to ensure the success and prosperity of not only the company, but the American economy," he wrote on Truth Social.

Nvidia said its leadership had received input from "senior levels of the United States government and was "confident that Mr Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk".

#185. Wealthy investors seek tax savings from municipal bonds as yields remain elevated

The business of tax-loss harvesting is booming with wealthy investors pouring billions of dollars into products like tax-aware long-short strategies. As 10-year Treasury yields have climbed to levels not seen since 2007, tax-savvy clients are also turning to municipal bonds to keep more of what they earn.

"High-net-worth investors, because of all the noise in direct indexing and tax-aware long-short strategies, have begun to expect tax management from all of their investments, including their fixed income," said Gavin Romm, senior vice president and head of separately managed accounts at AllianceBernstein.

While tax-loss harvesting can be done with other types of fixed income, high-income investors typically gravitate to municipal bonds for their tax-exempt income. Separately managed accounts, known as SMAs, held about $1.6 trillion in municipal bonds at the end of 2025, a 44% jump since 2017, per JPMorgan's estimate.

For top-bracket investors, municipal bonds can offer a twofold benefit: tax-equivalent yields that approach long-run stock-market returns, and losses that may offset gains elsewhere in a portfolio, according to Ran Leshem, BlackRock's head of SMA solutions. BlackRock, which manages $42 billion in muni SMAs, estimates that assets across muni SMAs have grown by 10% industry-wide over the past three years.

"You can get a tax-equivalent yield of over 7% that is becoming quite compelling for a lot of clients," said Leshem. "We are seeing yields that haven't been around for quite a few years, so it's a good opportunity for clients to lock in yields at these higher rates."

Rising yields earlier this fall pushed bond prices down, leaving some investors with paper losses, Leshem said. However, tax-loss harvesting can be harder with bonds than with stock, particularly municipal bonds, he noted. The municipal bond market is highly fragmented with more than 50,000 issuers, and it costs more and takes longer to transact, he said.

"There might not always be liquidity or someone willing to buy it for a fair price," Leshem said.

That said, bonds can offer a distinct tax advantage from equities, according to Romm.

By selling a stock at a loss, an investor can reduce the current tax bill by offsetting gains elsewhere. In exchange, the investor faces a potentially larger taxable gain if they later sell the replacement stock.

However, if an investor sells a bond at a loss and buys a replacement bond for more than its par value (or face value) the replacement bond's tax basis is generally adjusted down over time as it approaches maturity, Romm said. If the investor holds it to maturity, the amount repaid may match that adjusted basis, leaving no capital gain or loss on the replacement bond, he added.

"Tax-loss harvesting with equities, by definition, is tax deferral," he said. "With bonds, when done thoughtfully, it is actually tax avoidance."

That said, investors must be careful not to violate the IRS wash-sale rule by buying a "substantially identical asset" within 30 days before or after the sale.

That tax benefit gives fixed income another job beyond providing income and stability, Romm said.

"These aren't really positioned as get-rich strategies. They're more of stay-rich strategies," he said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#182. Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Nvidia's market value rose by nearly $1 trillion as its share price skyrocketed after the tech firm reinstated Jensen Huang as its chief executive with immediate effect.

His shock reinstatement comes less than two weeks after Huang resigned after admitting an affair with a woman who was alleged to be a Chinese spy, a period in which shares in the chipmaker have also fallen significantly.

Nvidia shares rose by more than 31% which also lifted stock markets, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq climbing by 6.5% and the S&P 500 also rose 4.75%.

Tech stocks more broadly have also rallied following large falls on Friday, with much of the rally starting in pre-market trading before Huang's return was announced.

Other chipmakers experienced large increases following Monday's announcement from the Nvidia board. Shares in Micron Technology jumped 19%, while AMD shares rose nearly 7%.

Tesla shares also lept up by nearly 11.5%, while other tech gains were more muted by comparison: Amazon shares were up nearly 3.5%, and Apple and Microsoft rose by less than 2%.

While the surge in Nvidia shares was large and drove its market value back towards $4tn, the firm's share price remains about 21% down from a month ago.

A sharp drop occurred on 4 November, after the Democrat's midterm election victory sparked fears of tougher AI regulation to come led to a broad tech stock sell-off.

On Friday alone, Nvidia shares fell by 13.7% as tech stocks more broadly fell, and as Reuters reported - unconfirmed by the BBC - that the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) over its accounting practices.

Nvidia is a key firm at the core of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, with the chips it makes central to the industry.

Nvidia's board of directors voted unanimously to reinstate the firm's co-founder following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him, the company said - but US President Donald Trump has claimed he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang.

Kathleen Brooks, research director at brokerage firm XTB, said Huang's return showed Huang was the "godfather" of AI, and "has a talismanic influence over the sector".

"Barely two weeks ago this man was ousted from the company that he founded due to serious misconduct and potential national security issues," she said.

"Even though Huang did not hold special voting shares on the board, Nvidia's directors cannot see a future for the company without him."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said Huang's reinstatement was an astonishing about-turn for Nvidia, particularly given reports of an as-yet unconfirmed SEC investigation.

"This is the biggest corporate U-turn in living memory. To go from being embroiled in a spy scandal to being reinstated as the head of one of the world's most valuable companies in less than a fortnight is nothing short of remarkable," he said.

"Today's stock market rebound represents a massive sigh of relief by investors. However, one cannot rule out a more conservative approach by Huang going forward.

"His every move will be scrutinised by the market, and he may wish to tread more carefully to protect his integrity and ensure that Nvidia is being run responsibly."

Politicians are also demanding greater scrutiny.

Senator Jeanne Shaheen, the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, said Huang's reinstatement "reportedly at the encouragement of Trump Administration officials and without any apparent accounting or response to the risks to our national security, demands an explanation."

She added: "What is abundantly clear is that the AI industry requires guardrails and oversight."

#178. BBC secret filming shows dog breeder selling sick puppies for £2k each

BBC secret filming shows dog breeder selling sick puppies for Â£2k each

When Katie Thornton paid Â£2,000 for a nine-week-old puppy for her son, she knew it was a premium price - but was happy to be doing business with a "legit", council-approved breeder.

But Ozzy the Dobermann pup started coughing in the car on the way home, and within 48 hours he was in a veterinary hospital receiving urgent treatment for pneumonia.

Since then, his ongoing health problems mean Katie has racked up more than Â£5,000 in vet bills.

A BBC investigation has found the breeder had been stripped of his five-star rating two months before selling several sick puppies, but continued to advertise as five-star.

We have also found at least 15 other breeders in England advertising puppies alongside claims their star rating is higher than it really is.

An expert reviewing our findings - including undercover footage we recorded at the breeder's home - said the investigation exposes a nationwide "wild west" of dog licensing, making it "easy for consumers to be scammed".

The breeder has not responded to our request for comment.

Katie Thornton has spent more than Â£5,000 on vet bills since buying Ozzy earlier this year

When Katie visited the premises of Mikjulora Dobermanns in Whitby, North Yorkshire, she said the puppies' mother "didn't look very well" and was "skinny" and "shaking".

But she felt the breeder, Michael Estill, was "pushing me to buy Ozzy" so she paid for him and took him home on the spot.

Katie defended her decision because she had done extensive research to check the business was "legit".

The mother-of-one, from Harrogate, said she had checked his licence number and read through his website and online adverts, which said he had decades of experience and a "five-star" breeding licence.

In England, local councils grant breeders in their area a licence and star rating dependent on welfare standards.

Vet reports for North Yorkshire Council from August and December 2025, seen by the BBC, cited dozens of concerns with Estill's business, including:

"Poor" living conditions "akin to a prison", with dogs kept in cages, stacked on top of each other, that were too small for them to turn around in

Deteriorating health and welfare of "sad" dogs

Puppies for sale not matching descriptions - "in other words it is a scam"

Prospective buyers "very vulnerable to unsuspectingly purchasing unhealthy puppies"

The business was "more or less a puppy farm masquerading as an honest and conscientious breeder. This must stop"

Estill "bullied" a local vet to perform an "unnecessary" Caesarean section on one of his dogs, to reduce the time he would have to spend monitoring her

His "demanding" behaviour led the practice to give him several doses of a powerful stimulant which he gave to one of his dogs at home - which the vet considered to be "negligent"

He had a three-year-old dog euthanised because he suspected her to have a neurological condition, despite the vet encouraging him to get diagnostic tests and specialist treatment first.

Following the reports, which concluded that the business was "functioning outside the law", the council renewed Estill's licence in January but downgraded his rating from five-star (the highest standards) to one-star (minor failings).

Dr Caroline Allen, founder of Pet Lighthouse and former RSPCA chief veterinary officer, said she was "surprised and shocked" that the business kept its licence.

A North Yorkshire Council spokesperson said the threshold for revoking a licence "was not considered to have been met on the evidence available".

They added: "We take animal welfare very seriously and investigate concerns brought to our attention.

"Following the veterinary inspection reports, we have continued to work with the licence holder to address the identified concerns and are satisfied that standards have significantly improved, resulting in a higher level of animal welfare at the premises."

The BBC has evidence that Estill continued to sell puppies while claiming on his website and in person that he was five-star rated.

While Estill has since updated his website homepage, he has not updated the metadata, which means when his business is searched for online the five-star claim still appears alongside a link to his site.

There is no official list of licensed breeders and their star ratings, and not all councils publish them, so Katie took his word for it.

"If I would've known he was a one-star breeder, I would've never even gone to the property. I wouldn't have bought a dog off a one-star breeder," she said, adding that she wanted to see Estill's licence revoked.

Kat Durrant's dog Bentley died aged three in 2022 of a heart condition known as dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM).

Within days of bringing him home from Mikjulora Dobermanns, Kat says Bentley was "really poorly".

"The vet asked me if I had got the dog from a puppy farm because of his condition," she added.

Three years later, Kat had Bentley put to sleep as his weak heart was "giving up" and he was struggling to breathe.

"Bentley suffered his whole life because of everything he'd been through when he was young," she said.

Kat, from Manchester, set up a Facebook group where other people who had bought dogs from Estill could share their experiences.

Including Kat, owners of 17 dogs mentioned in the group said the pets had died before the age of eight, including 10 below the age of four.

Several others claimed their dogs were sick at a young age but survived.

The vet report from December 2025 said Estill was not correctly testing his dogs for DCM despite his website saying that he did test.

The report said the fact that Estill did "nothing" when customers told him about DCM diagnoses was "truly the hallmark of a breeder who does not care about the welfare or improving the breed".

Kat Durrant's dog Bentley died after being diagnosed with a heart condition

Michelle Paton, who lives in Edinburgh, bought her puppy Cruz a week before Katie bought Ozzy.

He had a cough "as soon as he came through the front door" and was diagnosed with pneumonia and possible heart failure a few weeks later.

Michelle was frustrated to discover Kat's Facebook group and felt Estill's licence should have been revoked "years ago".

"If somebody was actually doing their job properly then they would have put an end to him breeding a long time ago," she said.

Caroline Housam, from York, bought her puppy Roxy the day before Katie bought Ozzy.

Roxy started coughing in the car on the journey home.

When Caroline took Roxy to the vet she said she was told there were problems with her glands, and later they found she had a limp and ulcers in her eyes.

She is having ongoing treatment and tests.

"I'm actually angry because I look at my dog and I'm thinking, 'I don't know what lies ahead'," said Caroline.

"When you listen to what other people have said [on Facebook] who have had [dogs] bred from him, her chances of living until two are slim."

Caroline Housam's pup Roxy has ongoing problems with her eyes

To corroborate the claims made, the BBC sent undercover reporters to film at Estill's home, posing as potential puppy buyers.

Estill refused to show us where the puppies and their mother slept.

He said he had "another 15 Dobermanns" in cages in a back room and worried that the neighbours would complain if we went in and they started barking.

He offered to show us the space another day and said he had "nothing to hide".

Reviewing our footage, Allen said this refusal was the "most serious concern" and breached regulations.

She added that the eyes of two of the puppies for sale were "ânot all normal", citing "a number of abnormalities" such as discoloration and the shape of the eye socket.

Looking at a photo of a male pup taken by the BBC, she said: "That is not a healthy dog."

Estill's licence certificate and star rating were also not visibly on display anywhere, despite government rules saying both should be "clearly and prominently displayed" for customers.

An independent vet told the BBC the breeder should not have sold this puppy as healthy because it has eye "abnormalities"

BBC research has found at least 15 other breeders in England who are also advertising puppies alongside claims their star rating is higher than the one they have been given by their local authority.

We checked through a list of 806 breeders with a rating between one and four stars.

Of the 573 breeders rated three-star or below (meaning lower standards), we could not find mention of a rating on their websites, social media pages or in online adverts for puppies in 541 cases.

Of the 32 who did mention a rating, 11 had active adverts claiming to be more highly rated than they really were.

Of the 45 four-star rated breeders to mention a rating, four claimed they were five-star in active adverts.

Drugs running was like 'a paper round' for exploited son, mum says

The man accused of tricking his friends out of Â£50,000

Ex-workers tell of 'chaotic atmosphere' at new Â£400m prison

One council confirmed a breeder we enquired about did not have a licence at all, another said they could not find the information we needed, and others took weeks to respond.

The list of breeders was provided by Paws on the Doors, an organisation aiming to collate breeders' star ratings across the country in one place. For each of the breeders whose online claims did not match the Paws on the Doors list, we checked with individual councils to verify the up-to-date rating.

Caroline says she feels "angry" and fears Roxy might not live past two years old

Commenting on the BBC's findings, Paws on the Doors co-founder Leigh Grant said the main reason "the scammers exist" was that central government and local councils had "failed" to properly implement the principles of legislation for breeder licensing from 2018.

He said a lack of support for animal welfare had led to a position where breeders can "falsely claim higher standards, without fear of detection".

Allen said our findings showed puppy breeding was "an absolute wild west" and there should be "countrywide teams of inspectors and vets who are properly trained to understand the legislation".

She said: "It is easy for consumers to be âscammed, âbut unfortunately at the heart of this there are really vulnerable animals and puppies."

Michelle Paton's Dobermann pup Cruz, pictured with her other dog Lupo

Amy Thompson-Copsey, senior public affairs officer at Dogs Trust, said potential puppy owners should "trust your instinct" to avoid falling for "financial scams".

"If something seems off, if you feel red flags, just walk away. You don't know what you could be getting into."

A Defra spokesperson said they were committed to ending "low welfare dog breeding" and were consulting on reforms to protect dogs' health and better inform the public on "which breeders they can trust".

The BBC put all allegations to Michael Estill and his wife Julie Estill, who is the named director of Mikjulora on Companies House, but they did not respond.

#175. The shake-up facing renters as a new kind of landlord emerges

The shake-up facing renters as a new kind of landlord emerges

On Christmas Eve 2021, there was a knock on the door of Lexi Levens' rented flat. It was a letter sent by special delivery from the NHS nurse's landlord saying she, her husband, and four children had two months to move out.

"The kids all thought it was a present arriving," she recalls.

Lexi's landlord had used something known then as a Section 21 notice to evict her family without needing to give a reason.

Lexi, along with the housing charity Shelter, went on to lobby the government to improve rights for renters. The Renters' Rights Act, which came into force in May this year, banned Section 21 evictions and also made it illegal to refuse to rent to people based on their status as a family with children.

The landlords Lexi had been renting from at that time were typical of the English housing market - individuals who owned one or a handful of properties.

Lexi Levens received a letter on Christmas Eve 2021 telling her that her family had to move out

Many of these small landlords say the Renters' Rights Act has left them feeling squeezed. They also say rising interest rates, new regulations and tax changes (including some in the most recent Budget) are making things harder. Some say they are selling up.

Meanwhile, according to Savills estate agents, 2026 is likely to be another record year for investment from large institutions in what is known in the property industry as build to rent (BTR). This is when big developers build blocks of flats or family housing to rent out, something that is commonplace in parts of Europe and the US. It means that rather than dealing with an individual landlord or letting agent, tenants are instead renting from larger, typically corporate, entities.

So does the shift, as some believe, represent a welcome professionalisation of the UK rental sector? Or will renters increasingly miss the positive elements of the personal touch that small landlords can provide?

According to the latest government survey of private landlords in England, 83% of English landlords own four properties or fewer, representing 51% of tenancies.

And according to 2024 research by real estate agency Savills, around 40% rental homes in the US and Germany are owned by large institutions - be they private firms, listed companies, pension funds, or co-operatives. For the UK, Savills calculated that figure was just 3%.

But the rental market is changing. The trend is most visible in England, where BTR has boomed in large cities and where regulations like the Renters' Rights Act apply, but there are BTR developments in Scotland, Wales, and Northern Ireland too and the devolved nations are also seeing increased regulation on landlords, such as with Scotland's rent controls.

Lexi is pleased with the idea of a sector with stricter rules for landlords and more professional institutions getting involved.

"Anything that takes away from landlords having personal preferences [around choosing who they are or not prepared to rent to]â¦ is a good thing," she says, adding that she believes large, corporate landlords would be less likely to act that way.

Not everyone is convinced BTR landlords are better, though.

Clint Stevenson, a 44-year-old magician for weddings and events, has been renting his one-bedroom flat in Ramsgate for Â£550 a month for two years. He says he has "the most amazing landlady" - an older woman who he regularly chats to. He sometimes walks her dog and does her shopping as well.

He says he had considered renting from a BTR development, but found the accommodation to be "very industrial and very unpersonal". He also found what was on offer to be expensive compared to what he is used to paying.

"They just look like big, money-grabbing deals," he says.

Clint Stevenson sometimes walks his landlady's dog and does her shopping too

In Cardiff, Grainger, the UK's largest listed BTR developer, is currently offering a fully-furnished studio flat on the 20th floor of The Copper Works in the city centre with views over the bay for Â£1,265 a month.

It's a lot more than what is currently on offer on Rightmove, a 15 minute walk away from the city centre - a fully-furnished, ground-floor, one-bedroom flat with a garden for Â£800 a month.

But tenants in The Copper Works have free access to a gym, a cinema room, a roof terrace, and a games room. There's also free WiFi, a concierge service with a helpline, and other amenities. In addition, the studio flat has an EPC B rating as opposed to an EPC C rating for the one-bedroom flat down the road.

"You have to look at what you're getting for your rent," says Helen Gordon, Grainger's chief executive. She says BTR landlords can offer these services because of their scale. As she puts it: "If you run 11,000 homes, you can afford to invest in systems, processes, people, helplines â that sort of thing â that smaller landlords can't."

Gordon strongly pushes back against suggestions that there is a lack of human connection within BTR, noting that tenants are able to speak with staff whenever they need and that the firm regularly organises events for socialising with other tenants to create a sense of community.

It's a pattern across the BTR sector: a higher price point in exchange for what is marketed as high-quality accommodation and a suite of services - some in the sector even refer to 'customers' rather than 'tenants'.

The number of BTR homes has tripled between 2019 and 2025, and while it represents just 3% of the overall rental market at the moment, Gordon believes it could reach "at least 10%" over the next ten years. "And I think it could go higher," she adds.

"If you've got major institutions that include pension funds, financial institutions, and FTSE 250 companies running the stock, it's easier to hold people to account than lots of disparate people, so you can see why it's attractive [to government]."

A 2012 government-commissioned report concluded that more BTR properties could help increase housing supply and drive economic growth as well as providing tenants with more choice and better quality housing. It recommended central government and councils support the development of BTR through favourable planning decisions and releasing public land.

There is a lot of evidence governments and local authorities listened. Local leaders in big cities â London and Manchester, in particular â have partnered with developers to deliver large housing projects involving, in many cases, the release of public land. Kings Cross in London is a prime example of this and current London Mayor Sadiq Khan's push for BTR development near Transport for London (TFL) land, mostly Tube stations, is another example. As part of that, TfL formed a joint venture with Grainger.

Lucian Cook, head of residential research at Savills, says it's not a matter of looking at one policy, government, or council in particular but more the "policy environment" which has been around since the 2010s.

He points to a series of tax changes, including mortgage interest tax relief changes and higher stamp duty rates for second homes and buy-to-let properties introduced by the Conservatives around 10 years ago, which made the sector less favourable for smaller landlords.

The Renters' Rights Act and more stringent energy efficiency requirements were later introduced. And the last two Budgets have raised taxes on rental income from next year. The tax change applies to individuals who are landlords, not to BTR companies which pay corporation tax, further encouraging professionalisation of the sector.

Yet, while many welcome these changes, others say they are pushing smaller landlords out of the sector. As Cook puts it, there has been more "stick" for small landlords than "carrot" for the BTR sector.

Between 2000 and 2016, a net 2.74 million homes were added to the English rental market in total, according to an analysis of government data by the Joseph Rowntree Foundation. In the 10 years since, a net of just 200,000 have. For renters, it is now less likely that your property is owned by a smaller landlord.

Kevin Barber started off with one rental home. He has since grown this portfolio to 40 properties with his business partner but resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector. "The big property companies have got so much financial power and influence with the government," he says.

Many experts, landlord groups, and tenant groups agree that building more housing - whether that's rental housing or housing for sale - would help solve many of the issues in the rental sector.

The thinking goes that building more homes for rent, provided people can afford them, would help deal with the supply crunch that can drive up rental costs and force tenants into lower quality properties. And building more homes for sale, provided people can afford them, would remove those from the market who don't want to rent but are forced into it.

Kevin Barber resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector.

But whether we will actually see more homes being built is another matter. Savills' Cook says BTR development could slow in future as high interest rates and increased building costs continue to hit developers' bottom line. These same factors are why housebuilders say they are building less.

Cook says the current rate of BTR development won't be enough to meet the demand for rental properties on its own, at least not imminently. In addition, while some BTR developers do build cheaper family homes for rent in the suburbs, the sector is largely still building blocks of flats for wealthier renters living in cities.

This raises the possibility that the UK could end up with a two-tier rental market â what Cook describes as the "haves and the have nots" â where high-earning city-dwelling renters have their pick of rental properties but poorer renters in suburbs struggle to find somewhere to live.

Unless the government steps in, possibly by delivering more housing itself or partnering with developers to do so, it might find any positives gained by the Renters' Rights Act are undone by an increasingly fragmented rental market.

The school where white working-class pupils defy the odds

Thatcher's Right to Buy created a generation of homeowners - did it also fuel the housing crisis?

#172. 10-year Treasury yield slides despite sticky inflation data as investors buy bonds for protection

U.S. Treasury yields fell on Wednesday, with investors fleeing to bonds for protection after October's personal consumption expenditures price index showed that inflation continued to accelerate.

The 10-year Treasury note yield — the main benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — was more than 3 basis points lower at 4.866%.

The yield on the 2-year Treasury note, which typically moves in line with short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was down more than 2 basis points at 4.65%.

The longer-dated 30-year Treasury yield, which tends to track geopolitical events, was more than 3 basis points lower at 5.254%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The personal consumption expenditures price index — the Federal Reserve's preferred gauge of underlying inflation — rose 3.8% year over year for October, in line with the Dow Jones consensus estimate. Separately, economic growth came in much stronger for the third quarter. Gross domestic product rose at a 4.3% annualized rate, according to a revised Commerce Department estimate.

The data reinforced expectations for another Fed rate hike in December.

"[I]t is more urgent than ever that Fed officials step up the pace of interest rate hikes to cool off economic demand," said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS. "Rate hikes are coming. Lots of them. Bet on it. The economy and inflation are literally on fire."

Wednesday's drop comes after yields steadied on Tuesday, with traders digesting softer demand for a 5-year Treasury auction.

Trading is shortened this week due to the Thanksgiving holiday. Bond markets will be closed all day Thursday for Thanksgiving, and will close at 2 p.m. E.T. on Friday.

#171. World Cup drove profits surge, says Marston's

The New Spires in Coventry was one of the busiest Marston's pubs in the country during this summer's World Cup

Pub company Marston's has said an "excellent World Cup" helped drive stronger trading as annual profits rose by nearly a fifth, despite reporting a dip in sales.

The Wolverhampton-based firm, which operates more than 1,300 pubs across the UK, reported underlying pre-tax profits of Â£85.4m for the year to 26 September, up 18.4% from a year earlier.

The company said trading was improved by new-look pubs, strong trade during this summer's football World Cup, and a partnership with Jeremy Clarkson's Hawkstone drinks brand.

"For the third year running, profit is significantly up, cash flow is strong and margins have improved," said chief executive Justin Platt.

The pub chain also said bookings for Christmas events were 9% ahead of the same point last year.

Marston's has invested heavily in refurbishing pubs into new formats, including its sports-focused "Grandstand" venues.

It plans at least 100 more conversions in the new financial year, in a bid to accelerate sales growth further.

One of the busiest locals during the World Cup was The New Spires in Coventry, which reopened in March following a Â£400,000 makeover.

The New Spires in Coventry reopened in March following a Â£400,000 conversion to a "Grandstand" venue

The World Cup had been "very full on," said pub partner Pip Sullivan, explaining that on one match night alone they took more than Â£25,000 over the bar.

Marston's said it had selected the venue for investment because of its involvement with local sports teams, community groups, and charities.

"We've got two football teams, four darts teams and a dominoes team," explained Sullivan, all of which had also helped bolster sales.

But she recognised not all businesses were thriving.

"It's been a difficult year for a lot of pubs and we have empathised as they've lost a lot of trade," she said.

But investment by the company had brought the pub "back to life", she continued.

"We've managed to keep as much custom as we possibly can and seen new custom."

A partnership with Jeremy Clarkson also helped drive profits, said the company

Marston's also pointed to events and promotions, including darts champion Luke Humphries' darts nights and the partnership with Clarkson.

"Guest satisfaction levels have improved again, thanks to the amazing work of our pub teams, whose passion and commitment keep our pubs at the heart of every local community they serve," added Platt.

"Our new pub formats are performing especially well and are now a proven growth engine for the business, giving us real confidence to accelerate our investment plans in 2027."

#170. Kalshi traders say state data center moratoriums are more likely than not

On Wednesday morning, Kalshi traders put the probability above 55% that at least four states would adopt data center moratoriums this year.

After Democrats swept Congress and key governor races during the midterm elections, Kalshi traders put the odds that at least four states would pause data center construction at about 75%. Since Nov. 22, the probability that at least four states would adopt moratoriums rose again, to 57% from 52%.

The contract asks "How many states will adopt data center moratoriums this year?" and will be verified from state governments.
