#168. Trump faces backlash after saying he 'convinced' Nvidia to reinstate CEO Jensen Huang

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang has been reinstated as the boss of Nvidia less than two weeks after he was forced to resign following the revelations of extra marital affairs, one of which was with a suspected Chinese spy.

The AI chipmaker handed its co-founder his job back, despite rejecting his initial offer to return, after pressure from US President Donald Trump.

Trump said he "made the deal" with the tech giant's board to get Huang his job back, but both the president and Nvidia are facing backlash from lawmakers.

But his comeback added almost $1 trillion to Nvidia's market value as its share price jumped 31%, ending ended a streak of losses on the US stock market.

Nvidia's gains helped boost shares in other tech companies and financial markets in Asia while European markets were flat.

However, despite Huang's return, Nvidia's share price remains down over the past month amid investor concerns about the wider AI industry and future development.

But it is a dramatic return for the man dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" to the company at the centre of the AI spending boom.

Attention will soon turn to Huang's appearance before Congress next week, where the Nvidia boss will be questioned over his relationship with Alice Huang, alleged to be a spy for China, due to national security concerns.

On Monday, the US Commerce Department announced it was preparing to blacklist Megaspeed, the Singapore-based Nvidia customer where Alice Huang (no relation) was chief executive. China has denied the spying allegations.

Harvey Jones, chairman of Nvidia's board, said Huang's reinstatement after originally resigning on 12 November was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

Last Tuesday, the board explicitly said it did not intend to reinstate him following calls to do so and Huang himself stating he would be open to returning.

Asia markets jump as Nvidia boss Jensen Huang reinstated after spy scandal

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

The U-turn suggests Nvidia's board believe Huang is irreplaceable.

The 63-year-old, who co-founded the company in 1993, transformed it from a chipmaker for video and computer games to the semiconductor powerhouse it is today, making the chips used in AI systems.

It is now one the most valuable firms in the world. Some believed Huang's departure risked not only the future of Nvidia, but the wider AI industry given the position the company, under Huang, has in the financing of other tech firms.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

In a interview with CNBC on Monday, Trump said he had spoken to Nvidia's board of directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

"They did ask for assurance on the national security threat," Trump said. "And we gave them assurances at least with everything we know. And we know a lot."

"People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it's a threat to lose him," the president added.

The president was asked whether Nvidia stock losses after Huang resigned last week had influenced his decision to personally intervene.

Since returning last year to the White House, the president has amassed a significant level of investment in Nvidia.

Recent financial disclosures show he purchased $265,000 in its stock and sold as much as $15,000 worth of shares in September.

A BBC review of his government financial disclosures for the last two years shows he purchased shares of Nvidia shares worth between $23m and $86m (Â£18m and Â£66m). And he has bought Nvidia stock 85 separate times through a number of investment accounts.

The value of those holdings would have been affected by the recent fall in Nvidia's stock price amid Huang's exit.

Trump has also sold some of his stake in Nvidia since returning to the presidency. Over 19 sales, Trump gained between $3.5m and $10.2m, according to his government disclosures.

Both Trump and White House have repeatedly rejected assertions that he has profited from the presidency. The president has said "everybody" was making money from rising stocks and he does not make decisions related to his investment accounts.

The White House has emphasised that Trump has placed his businesses in a trust managed by his sons.

The rise and fall and rise again of Jensen Huang

The head of the International Association for Safe and Ethical AI, Prof Stuart Russell, told the BBC that the Nvidia board must have been under "enormous pressure" from the White House to reinstate Huang.

Concerns remain among US lawmakers, including some Republicans, over Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US export controls.

Hakeem Jeffries, who is expected to lead the House of Representatives as Speaker when Democrats take control in January, called the decision to reinstate Huang "indefensible, irresponsible and incomprehensible".

"We will not allow America's national security to be sold out by the Trump Administration for personal financial gain," he said.

Huang is scheduled to appear before Congress for a closed-door interview with three House committees on 1 December. Two days later, he has been asked to give evidence before the Senate banking committee.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, who is set to be chairwoman of the Senate Banking Committee in January, said the reinstatement was "reckless".

And the chair of the Senate's select committee on intelligence, Republican Tom Cotton said: "The controversy with Nvidia and Megaspeed is just the tip of the iceberg, and it's not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back."

#165. Prediction markets push OpenAI IPO expectations later into 2027

Anthropic's shaky performance on the public markets since its debut last week and a continuing tech sell-off may be changing the outlook for when prediction markets think OpenAI will go public.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi think there's a roughly 28% chance the ChatGPT developer officially announces an IPO by June 2027. That figure was most recently above 50% earlier this month.

Even by October 2027, odds are still only 50-50 that the private company — most recently conducting a funding round at a $1.25 trillion valuation — will make its move to the public markets.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 form as effective, the IPO being priced or an exchange has assigned a ticker to the company. Contracts are resolved using reports from media outlets.

OpenAI confidentially filed for an IPO in June, but then in an interview with Fortune published in September CEO Sam Altman ruled out a 2026 public markets debut as questions over artificial intelligence safety grew.

The company's chief rival, Anthropic, debuted on the public markets last week. However, the stock has declined nearly 30% from its peak during its first trading session, and on Tuesday it closed below its IPO price of $83.

Meanwhile, a perpetual futures contract for OpenAI on Hyperliquid has tumbled more than 30% since highs in early October. In late October, OpenAI pared back its goal for its recent funding round to $20 billion from $30 billion.

#164. Will I get the £100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Up to 20 million households in England will get a Â£100 payment to help with their energy bills this winter, Chancellor John Healey has announced in his Budget.

Here's what you need to know about the payment.

For many people, the Â£100 on offer is a lot of money, but it's worth considering the context.

A typical energy bill is forecast to jump by Â£443 a year from January, according to consultants Cornwall Insight.

Therefore many people will still feel worse off despite the payment.

Those living in properties which are in council tax bands A to D in England will get the payment, the government has said.

As such, it's worth checking which council tax band your home is in, external. If you live in England, you can use the search function by typing your postcode into the box.

If you get a paper council tax bill, your band will also be printed on there.

The chancellor said the policy was for England but that funding would be given to Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland to make the payment as well.

The Scottish and Welsh governments have confirmed they will use the funding to introduce equivalent or similar payment schemes.

The Northern Irish government does not have the legal authority to make the Â£100 payments unless it agrees its budget, which it has failed to do since the start of this financial year.

Your pay, energy bills and savings: How the Budget affects you

If you pay your council tax by direct debit, the money will be paid directly into your bank account.

Those who don't pay by direct debit will be contacted by their council and given the choice to receive the Â£100 directly into their bank account or as credit on their council tax bill.

The government will ask councils to make payments from 4 January, and the scheme will be open until the end of May.

Students living in halls will not get the payment because student halls are not in council tax bands A to D.

However, if you're a student living in a property that is in council tax bands A to D, as in a private home or flat let out by a landlord, then you will get the payment.

Students do not pay council tax so the local authority will be responsible for finding a way to give them the payments.

This is also the case for those who do not pay council tax because of another kind of exemption, or if you do pay council tax but you do it by cash or some other way.

The government said it would give councils guidance on how local authorities should do this in due course.

The government has said the support is to help households with the cost of energy.

But the money will appear directly in people's bank accounts so you could choose to spend it on something else, while some people may choose to receive the Â£100 as a credit on their council tax bill.

The chancellor said the money for the Â£100 cash payments is coming from a tax rise on banks.

The surcharge on banks' corporation tax will rise from 3% to 6% for 15 months from 1 January 2027 to 31 March 2028.

#162. Rejecting cheap fabrics, higher prices: Why shoppers are asking for better clothes

Clothing isn't made like it used to be, and more shoppers are taking notice.

The makeup of apparel imported into the U.S. has shifted in recent years. In 2010, about 60% of clothing was predominantly made from cotton. Now, that total is around 45%, while predominantly synthetic fabrics such as polyester and nylon comprise the majority of imports.

Apparel prices generally declined over the two decades leading up to the pandemic, according to the consumer price index. Since 2020, they have soared to record highs.

It's starting to change habits for consumers who are paying more for food, gas, rent and other staples. As shoppers shell out more for clothes, they're increasingly making sure the garments are worth the cost.

Consulting firm AlixPartners' 2026 Consumer Sentiment Index, which surveyed more than 9,000 shoppers, found that consumers are scrutinizing labels more closely and rejecting cheap materials sold at a premium.

"If it's not a natural material, a retailer brand who passes it off as it is, they're really becoming very low on consumers' minds," said Sonia Lapinsky, head of fashion at AlixPartners.

Fast fashion brands like Forever 21 and H&M exploded in popularity during the early 2000s and 2010s, bringing trendy clothing to shelves quickly at low prices. Then came ultra-fast fashion, with brands like Shein and Fashion Nova pumping out new garments at even quicker speeds and lower price points — made possible in part through the use of inexpensive, synthetic fabrics.

"We got to a point where the pendulum just swung too far in that direction. And we've actually begun to see a reversal," said U.S. Bancorp BTIG consumer analyst Janine Stichter. "Quality matters a lot more ... over the last three or four years than it has in the previous 10 or 20."

But quality, which shoppers often tie directly to material, can come at a higher cost.

The average unit price of apparel imported to the U.S. between January and August was $2.64 per square meter for garments that were predominantly synthetic fiber, like polyester and nylon, compared with $3.67 for items that were largely cotton, according to data from the U.S. Commerce Department's Office of Textiles and Apparel.

When comparing those 2026 figures to 2010, synthetic fiber apparel import costs have climbed around 11%, while cotton apparel costs have risen 21%.

"If you're appealing to a more value-sensitive customer, you either reduce the quality, or you negotiate with suppliers to try to share some of the cost or you maybe absorb the cost … and perhaps hope to crank a higher volume of product to make up for it," said Bernstein Research senior retail analyst Aneesha Sherman.

Though polyester is cheaper, experts point out that's not necessarily a bad thing. It can help clothing hold its shape and wick away sweat.

Sherman believes the athleisure boom is a primary factor in the shift toward polyester. She said that from 2015 to 2025, sports apparel was growing at a 4.9% rate in the U.S., versus 2.6% for all apparel and footwear, according to Euromonitor data.

"There probably is some quality trade down as well happening there," Sherman said. "But I think both these trends are conflated."

Nonetheless, backlash to synthetic materials is growing. Gen Z has even repurposed the word "polyester" as an insult to mean generally low-quality or fake.

Concerns have also grown about the environment and human health surrounding petroleum-based clothing, as more research indicates microplastics can leach from polyester clothing when washed.

It's led consumers to reexamine what materials are in their clothing.

Apparel prices have risen coming out of the pandemic, as demand outpaced supply and allowed companies to raise prices, said BTIG's Stichter. Tariffs and the Iran war followed, raising costs for businesses — and prices for consumers.

Petroleum is the basis of many synthetic fibers, and oil prices have skyrocketed since the start of the Iran war.

The same factors that helped drive up prices of clothing — Covid, tariffs and war — also affected clothing quality.

"Companies had to move around their supply chains in the post-Covid period," said Stichter. "They had to find ways to take costs out, and sometimes that meant using less expensive fabrics."

Those factors compounded a more-than-decade-long shift away from manufacturing in China.

Sheng Lu, a fashion and apparel studies professor at the University of Delaware, whose research focuses on the economics of the global textile industry, said China's established history with clothing production means it has higher-skilled workers, state-of-the-art-equipment and better material management compared with emerging manufacturing destinations such as Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and Cambodia.

"They mostly serve the value market and the mass market. And I think the reason why they're not ready to make higher-quality luxury and premium products really reflects the kind of skill sets you can expect when you source from these countries," said Lu.

AlixPartners' Lapinsky said these countries that manufacture at a lower cost sometimes have to still ship the raw materials from China, which can extend the timeline to make clothing. That means taking earlier bets on trends and then discounting if products don't sell well.

"If you have to mark down that product because the consumer doesn't want it as much, your net margin might actually be worse than if you were to just invest in quality in the first place," said BTIG's Stichter.

And as material costs rise for synthetic fibers, Stichter also said she believes it could further influence companies to use more natural fabrics moving forward.

Companies prioritizing quality are winning in this environment.

Ralph Lauren's stock is up around 85% over the past two years. Around 90% of its apparel is primarily made of cotton, leather, wool, viscose, cashmere or silk, according to its 2026 sustainability report. In its fiscal 2026, the company said it sold more products at full price and reduced markdowns.

Meanwhile, direct-to-consumer clothing brand Quince, which centers its brand identity around natural materials, reached a $10 billion valuation this year.

The brand jumped 16 spots to No. 3 for best resale value in online consignment retailer ThredUp's 2025 report. Organic cotton retailer Pact jumped 20 spots on that list to No. 8, illustrating the potential role materials are playing in brand recognition.

Alternatively, the 2026 AlixPartners report, which ranks sentiment for brands in different retail categories, found shoppers are viewing some fast fashion brands less favorably. Both Shein and Zara dropped three spots — to fifth and seventh place, respectively — in terms of sentiment in the young adult specialty category.

Shein passed higher tariff costs onto shoppers in 2025 and then blamed a sales slowdown on those higher prices. The ultra-fast fashion retailer saw its year-over-year share of U.S. apparel, accessories and footwear card spending decrease in the fourth quarter of 2025 and first two quarters of 2026, according to Consumer Edge.

Shein did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Though Zara fell in AlixPartners' Consumer Sentiment Index, it has continued to grow sales and profits year over year. Even so, Lapinsky said worsening consumer views are something to watch, even if the company is doing well.

"What we found over the years with this consumer sentiment index is that sometimes it will be a bit of a leading indicator of what's to come," said AlixPartners' Lapinsky. "I'm not saying that this is going to be true for Zara, but I think that's one thing that we'll be watching as we look in the upcoming quarters and into 2027."

#161. Huawei found guilty of stealing US trade secrets but acquitted of racketeering

Defence lawyer Brian Heberlig makes closing arguments at the Huawei Technologies trial at Brooklyn federal court on November 17.

Huawei has been found guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets and bank fraud, but not guilty of the more serious overarching racketeering charge in a landmark case in the US.

In a two-month trial, prosecutors accused the Chinese technology and telecommunications giant of lying, cheating and stealing to gain any competitive advantage.

Huawei's defence team argued that prosecutors had decided Huawei was a criminal organisation and built its case from there, but the firm's success was due to its own innovation.

Huawei said, external the racketeering acquittal "confirms that these allegations lack factual basis", adding that it would "take all necessary legal actions to defend our rights and interests".

Jurors in a Brooklyn court found Huawei guilty of 10 out of 12 counts, which included conspiracy to violate US sanctions.

However, the jury declined to convict Huawei of the racketeering conspiracy charge, which required members to find the firm acted like a criminal enterprise.

Federal prosecutors first announced the charges against Huawei in 2019, after the dramatic arrest in late 2018 of senior executive Meng Wanzhou in Canada.

Meng, also known as Cathy Meng, was held for three years in Canada before she was released to return to China through a deal with US prosecutors.

During closing arguments last week, assistant US attorney Meredith Arfa told jurors Huawei spent 20 years doing whatever it took to expand its business, including lying to banks about its business dealings in Iran and stealing technology secrets from rival firms to ensure it had a competitive edge.

"For decades, Huawei and three of its subsidiaries conducted their business in the United States on the basis of lies and theft," US Attorney General Todd Blanche said in statement, external.

"Thanks to the work of the FBI and our federal prosecutors, those who engaged in these criminal activities will be held accountable."

The Chinese government hit out at what it called the US "suppression" of Chinese companies and said it supports Chinese firms protecting their interests.

Defence attorney Brian Heberlig said it was by dint of hard work that Huawei's business had expanded with operations in 170 countries and more than 200,000 staff.

Following the verdict, Heberlig told the BBC that Huawei was gratified that the jury had rejected the racketeering charge.

"The RICO [Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act] count that alleged that Huawei was a corrupt organisation, with a corporate culture and policy of committing crime. That was, in our view, a significant overreach, and the jury soundly rejected it," he said.

"Of course, we have mixed emotions about the rest of the verdict ... [we] thought that Huawei was deserving of acquittals across the board."

Sentencing is planned for September 2027. Heberlig said there will be "significant advocacy" to achieve the lowest sentence possible. Following that, he said he fully expects Huawei to appeal.

"If I know Huawei, that fight will continue, and the company's not going to rest until it's pursued every last argument it can through appeal, and hopefully, ultimately toward vindication," he said.

#158. Ex-GM CEO on Mary Barra succession planning: 'It's going to be tough'

Former General Motors CEO and Chairman Dan Akerson doesn't mince words when asked about the challenges the automaker faces in finding a successor for GM CEO Mary Barra: "It's going to be tough."

He should know. The 78-year-old businessman and U.S. Navy veteran helped with his own succession planning and recommended to GM's board in 2013 that Barra replace him as CEO. She has held the top job since Jan. 15, 2014.

Akerson said Barra will be hard to replace, citing her success and prominence as the first female CEO of any major automaker and the ongoing challenges facing the automotive industry, such as artificial intelligence, vehicle digitalization, and the rise of China and autonomous vehicles.

"It's going to be very, very dynamic," Akerson told CNBC during a phone interview. "It's going to be a big change."

Akerson declined to discuss potential internal candidates such as GM Chief Product Officer Sterling Anderson, CFO Paul Jacobson or President Mark Reuss, but he said the eventual successor needs to be dynamic and, if from the outside, "adaptable."

His comments to CNBC follow a Wall Street Journal report on Tuesday that GM is working with an executive search firm to identify possible outside candidates to succeed Barra, in addition to internal prospects. GM declined to comment on the report or Barra's succession.

Akerson, who came to GM as an outsider from The Carlyle Group in 2010, said selecting an outside candidate might be risky if there are already executives within the organization who have similar leadership and technical expertise.

"Is there anybody in the organization that has that background that you know better day to day over a period of time? You may be taking a risk by bringing in an outsider," he said.

The prospect of an outsider leading GM would be unusual in the auto industry, where major automakers have generally promoted internal CEO candidates, apart from periods of exceptional disruption such as the Great Recession.

Stellantis, BMW, Toyota Motor, Nissan Motor and Hyundai Motor have all appointed internal executives to their CEO positions in recent years.

Barra has said several times that her successor and her longevity in the role would be determined by the board.

A notable influence is likely Hewlett Packard veteran Patricia Russo, GM's longest-serving board member who has been on the board since 2009 and leads its CEO succession planning as independent lead director.

GM's CEO succession planning is done "at least annually," according to the company's yearly proxy filing. That process includes Barra providing the board "with recommendations for, and evaluations of, potential CEO successors and discusses developmental plans for these successors," according to the filing.

That is followed by board members engaging "with CEO candidates and senior management talent at Board and committee meetings and other forums to enable directors to personally assess candidates."

When Akerson was preparing to retire, Barra was among a pool of four candidates — two longtime GM employees and two relative newcomers — who were finalists for CEO in 2013, he said.

Akerson said he put the finalists through an extended evaluation, asking how they viewed GM's strengths and weaknesses, how they would organize the company, and their willingness to work together, potentially with other CEO candidates who didn't get the job.

Akerson said the final decision to appoint Barra was done at a day-and-a-half-long board meeting in Washington. The candidate field was narrowed to Barra and former GM executive Dan Ammann, an ex-Wall Street banker who was GM's CFO before leaving the automaker's Cruise unit, reportedly over a disagreement in strategy with Barra, in 2021.

"Mary's very deserving of all the accolades she's received," Akerson said. "She's done a terrific job."

Barra has been leading the automaker for nearly 13 years, which makes her the longest-serving CEO of the automaker since Alfred Sloan retired after a 23-year career as CEO in 1946.

Akerson said he believes that, like he did, Barra will have major influence in picking her successor. He described that dynamic as integral to continuing her legacy leading the automaker.

"This is important. I think if she doesn't pick the right person, in four or five years from now and things are back in the trough, that will tarnish her history. But I think she'll pick the right one," he said.

#157. US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

US Attorney General Todd Blanche has backed Nvidia as a "good corporate citizen" while refusing to comment on whether the AI chipmaker was under federal investigation.

Blanche said he had no information about newly reinstated CEO Jensen Huang's security clearance status, as lawmakers demand answers about US President Donald Trump's involvement in Huang's comeback.

Huang was given his job back less than two weeks after being forced to resign over revelations of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Asked several times on Tuesday whether the justice department or FBI had investigated Huang, Blanche responded: "I don't know what you're talking about."

Huang is still set to appear before Congress next week, where he will be questioned about national security concerns over his relationship with an alleged spy who previously ran Megaspeed, a Singapore-based corporate customer of Nvidia.

Trump has said he would consider pardoning Huang for any potential criminal activity, though he does not think it will come to that.

"It's always an option, I guess, but I don't think it'll be necessary," Trump told the New York Post in an interview published on Tuesday.

With Huang rescue, Trump riskily ties his political fortunes to Nvidia

The US commerce department has also announced it was preparing to blacklist the tech company Megaspeed, which has been suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

The commerce and justice departments said last week that they began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

But Blanche told reporters on Tuesday that the latter had "hundreds, if not more" export investigations occurring at any one time.

"So when you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about," he said.

Asked if he was satisfied Nvidia had been co-operative with inquiries into whether it or Huang knew about chips being diverted to China, Blanche said: "I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target or was subject to some criminal investigation or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that the president is saying to the American people.

"It shouldn't be what my message would be either."

While Nvidia's stock market value surged on the announcement of Huang's reappointment, some of the gains were reversed in volatile Tuesday trading.

The company's share price closed down nearly 6% - still well above where it ended last week. On Monday, Nvidia stock rocketed more than 31% following news of Huang's return.

Huang reportedly apologised to Nvidia employees on Tuesday, the day after returning to the company as chief executive, saying he had made "terrible mistakes" and was now receiving daily 90-minute therapy sessions.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

The company's board reinstated Huang on Monday in a shock U-turn. Harvey Jones, its chairman, said Huang's return was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

On Monday, Trump said he had spoken to the company's directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

Huang's reinstatement provoked bipartisan anger.

Republican Congressman Don Bacon said Nvidia should have "ignored the administration's inexplicable desire to save Huang".

Republican Senator Pete Ricketts said Huang's behaviour "doesn't absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips".

Democratic Congressman Robert Garcia, the incoming chairman of the House Oversight Committee, said Trump's "direct involvement" with Nvidia's decision "raises serious national security and corruption questions".

Democratic Senator Ruben Gallego said Congress needed to "investigate what stock positions took place before the announcement".

#155. Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Tuesday struck a conciliatory and remorseful tone while leading his first employee meeting since being reinstated, and said how sorry he was for what he had done.

Huang told employees that he let them down and promised to do a better job the second time around, according to people inside the room, who asked not to be named in order to share details. "I hurt all of you," Huang said, according to one of the people.

The CEO also addressed succession planning during the event, the people said. He told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.

A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to one of the people present.

Investors and analysts were left to speculate about the company's future leadership in the wake of Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.

On Monday, Huang was reinstated as CEO of the chipmaker by Nvidia's board of directors. Eleven days before, Huang had resigned from Nvidia after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said last week that Huang resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

Huang started the meeting by thanking CFO Colette Kress for her leadership as interim CEO.

Huang told staff that Tuesday's company meeting was the second hardest meeting of his life, the first one being with his wife. He said he is in therapy, according to one of the people present.

In a press release announcing Huang's return on Monday, the company said the board "received input" from senior officials in the federal government about any potential national security risk around the CEO. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he gave the board assurances and "made the deal" to restore Huang to his post.

After the company had its worst week in the stock market since 2008, the stock surged more than 30% the day his return was announced. Shares fell on Tuesday, however.

An Nvidia representative didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

#153. Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

As the market bell rang out at the Nasdaq today, the Nvidia super-bounce faded.

Its shares slid and are down almost 30% over the past six months, well before the scandal involving Jensen Huang emerged earlier this month.

The legitimate fears about the staying power of the tech boom pre-dated Huang's exit. They have not gone away with his return.

Investors are jittery. Already Anthropic is now trading notably lower than last week's stock market flotation. The firm is the first purely AI company to be publicly listed, and is being seen as a big test for continued investor appetite for the technology.

President Trump's extraordinary phone call to CNBC taking credit for telling the board to reinstate Huang showed the extent of the operation - of some in Silicon Valley and within the US administration - to get him back at the helm.

Among the truly astonishing reflections from President Trump was a suggestion that a two-day investigation of allegations was done and had cleared Huang of national security concerns.

The President said his remarkable move to intervene was because Huang is a "national treasure" who has the next generation of AI chips "in his head". Trump says this is why he needed to act.

In markets, those sceptical about the AI boom say they have never seen an operation to prop up a stock market boom like this. The bond vigilantes and short sellers are now up against Silicon Valley and the White House.

Investors are trading not revenue numbers but levels of political support at a time when US voters have just delivered a backlash against AI data centres.

Against this backdrop, the recovery of the central cash-generating foundation of the tech boom - Nvidia and its $400bn (Â£304bn) annual sales of advanced chips - looks politically fragile.

This worsens other uncertainties and risks, such as Nvidia's central role in the web of financing, borrowing and cross-ownership to support every corner of the build out of data centres.

The brief interim Nvidia chief executive Colette Kress had already signalled a rowing back from elements of this strategy last week. Where is that now?

The fall back in Anthropic's share price also raises questions about OpenAI's planned listing on the stock exchange. Ultimately these two companies might be the most critical part of all this.

They remain valuable companies with phenomenal technology relied on by the world's biggest companies. The real question is whether that reliance eases, as corporations eschew expensive frontier models for cheaper generic models offered by China.

In turn, all this could be affected by the political backlash, to which the Nvidia psychodrama now contributes.

It is simply not normal to have trillion dollar swings in the value of individual companies.

The extraordinary volatility shows the fragile mood of investors.

#150. Ukraine tells Putin to ‘stop complaining’ over oil refinery attacks as Russia promises retaliation

Ukrainian Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha has told Russian President Vladimir Putin to "stop complaining" over attacks on Russian refineries, saying Putin could end Kyiv's strikes on the oil facilities by bringing an end to the Kremlin's nearly five-year-long war.

His comments come shortly after Russia's president told a meeting of government officials and senior oil executives in the Kremlin on Tuesday that Ukraine continues to attack refining facilities despite U.S. President Donald Trump's appeal to refrain from doing so.

"I would like to note that retaliatory action from our side is inevitable. Everyone must be aware of that," Putin said, according to Russian state news agency Tass.

In September, Trump called on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy to stop targeting Russian oil refineries, saying the attacks were "hurting the world" as fuel supply disruptions from the wars in Ukraine and Iran propelled U.S. diesel prices to a record high. Moscow welcomed Trump's push for Ukraine to halt strikes on Russia's diesel-producing facilities at the time.

"If Putin doesn't like Ukraine's strikes on his oil refineries, he can order an end to the war," Ukraine's Sybiha said Tuesday via social media.

"If he doesn't want his military vessels hit, he can order an end to the war. Not to mention the 432,000 Russian soldiers he has lost on the battlefield this year without coming any closer to the goals of his invasion. Clearly, he doesn't care about them. Stop complaining. End the war," he added. CNBC could not independently verify the figures.

In July, the Center for Strategic and International Studies estimated that about 1.4 million Russian troops were killed, wounded or went missing between the start of the full-scale invasion in Feb. 2022 and June 2026, with more than 30,000 casualties a month in the first half of this year.

Ukraine has stepped up attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

Kyiv has also targeted Russian energy infrastructure. Zelenskyy said earlier this month that Ukraine's long-range strikes had disabled more than 55% of Russia's oil-refining capacity, describing this as a "justified response to Russia's refusal to de-escalate and its strikes on our energy infrastructure."

Ukrainian drones reportedly struck two major Russian oil refineries in Russia's Samara region overnight, triggering fires at both facilities.

Satellite images published in late October also showed black smoke rising from multiple fires at Rosneft's Novokuibyshevsk oil refinery in southern Russia following a suspected Ukrainian drone attack.

Putin on Tuesday said Russia's fuel market situation remains "challenging" and called on government officials and oil executives "to continue reacting promptly to the supply-demand relationship for fuel across the whole territory of Russia," according to Interfax.

Ukraine's allies have warned the country faces another winter of blackouts and freezing temperatures amid escalating Russian energy strikes.

#148. Trump's rescue of Nvidia's Huang brings political risks

Trump boosts Nvidia boss return but are US politicians on board?

After the AI chipmaker Nvidia announced it was bringing back embattled former head Jensen Huang and the company's stock shot up like a bullet, President Donald Trump took a very public victory lap.

"I made the deal," he said during a lengthy phone interview with cable business news channel CNBC.

Trump followed that with an AI-generated image posted to his social media platform that riffed on the famous photograph taken moments after the 2024 attempt on his life in Pennsylvania. But instead of Trump in the image, Huang was the one surrounded by Secret Service guards. It depicted him in front of an Nvidia banner - with a raised fist clenching a red bra.

The post was classic Trump â potentially offensive, yet still a bit opaque.

Was Trump suggesting Huang survived a scandal â one that includes allegations of recklessly jeopardising US national security by consorting with a suspected Chinese spy â the way he had survived an assassin's bullet?

Was the bra a celebration Huang's reported extramarital affairs? By replacing the US flag with an Nvidia standard, was he claiming that America and one of its largest, most powerful companies are one and the same?

At the very least, the image underscores how inextricably linked Huang and Trump now are, with the fate of the former hinging on the successes and failures of the latter.

And with that comes significant risks for the American president and for Republicans, including Vice-President JD Vance, who played a key part in his efforts to rescue Huang.

While Trump has said that a "very thorough investigation" by federal officials "exonerated" Huang and his reinstatement poses no national security threat, that is far from the final word on the matter.

"Every time you have a Chinese woman that's good looking or beautiful, they say she's a spy," Trump told CNBC, adding that "with Jensen, I don't believe that's true".

Democrats and some concerned Republicans in Congress have a different view.

They are promising to launch their own oversight of Huang and Nvidia, with subpoenas and compelled witness testimony, in order to assess whether they violated laws by allowing China to access sophisticated microchip technology.

State governments could also launch their own investigations.

Any new revelations could further damage the company's standing â and, by association, Trump himself.

"I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access," Republican Senator Tom Cotton posted on X.

"Congress has much to investigate," he said.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

What Jensen Huang's return teaches China's Xi about Trump

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

While Huang has a long record of success at Nvidia, there is no guarantee that he can recapture past glory now that his star his dimmed.

Even if he is free from any criminal liability, the tech mogul is about to be in a much harsher spotlight than he has ever experienced.

Trump also appears to be on the wrong side of public opinion when it comes to AI writ large.

In the recent US midterm elections, candidates on both the left and right campaigned on imposing new restrictions on data centre construction, with many calling for new legal guardrails to prevent AI-fuelled economic and social disruption.

"Rather than addressing the American people's growing concerns about AI, this administration remains asleep at the wheel or willfully ignorant as our most advanced technologies fall into the hands of our greatest competitors," congresswoman Sydney Kamlager-Dove, a California Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told the BBC.

The president's own political base is also suspicious of the new technology, with rural conservatives often rebelling at the demands that big tech companies have placed on their local resources and civic infrastructure.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

The Nvidia story has also exposed rifts in Trump's own administration, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, a China hawk, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, a vocal proponent of AI, publicly feuding over whether to impose new restrictions on AI chip exports.

Both Bessent and Lutnick travelled with Trump on his recent trip to China, although it was the commerce secretary who hitched a ride back to the White House on the Marine One helicopter when Trump returned to Washington last Thursday.

Polling also indicated that political corruption was a top issue for voters this year.

The president's willingness to intervene in the deliberations of a major private company in which he owns millions of dollars of stock could further fuel those concerns.

Trump's decision to help save Huang â noting on Monday that "we have to protect our geniuses, and he certainly is a genius" â could further inflame the public's sense that the rich and powerful, genius or not, play by a different set of rules.

"It almost feels like they've bent the rule, the regulation, to allow this to happen because it's such an important part of our economy right now," Steve Bannon, a former top Trump adviser, said on his podcast on Monday.

"Even in the crash of 2008... I never saw the government step in and take over a board of directors."

For this American president, however, these risks appear to be worth taking because of the stakes involved.

Trump has long viewed the success of the US economy as a key part of his political legacy.

He boasted of the historic growth and low unemployment during his first presidential term (while glossing over the disruptions caused by the Covid pandemic).

And since returning to office, he has celebrated as major US stock indices posted new record highs.

Recent US economic expansion has been fuelled largely by investment in AI technology, and Trump has become one of the industry's biggest boosters.

He scolded states and local governments for blocking data centre construction and predicted that the nation that wins the race for supremacy in AI â which he has tried to rebrand as "super intelligence" â will control the fate of the world.

If Trump views the economy as the key to his political success and AI as instrumental to the health of the US economy, then his dramatic, precedent-shattering actions to try to rescue Nvidia, the company at the centre of America's AI industry, make more sense.

In the weeks since concerns over Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy first surfaced, Nvidia's stock price tumbled more than 32%.

US stock indices tumbled as well, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq down 10% last week and the larger S&P 500 dropping 8.6%.

The recent financial turmoil may have given Trump a glimpse into a future where AI growth is just the latest financial bubble â like the housing market in 2008 and internet companies in 2001 â that is due for a resounding pop, with similarly dire economic consequences to follow.

Trump's intervention on behalf of Huang arrested that drop, at least for a day.

On Tuesday, Nvidia's stock price gave back much of the bounce it had enjoyed, and larger stock indices were mixed. The fate of the America economy and the AI juggernaut that has powered it is still undetermined.

Trump's intervention may not be a crisis fully averted, but only a crisis delayed.

And if an AI reckoning does come, the US president will be hard-pressed to avoid being caught in the maelstrom.

US to blacklist Asian AI firm Megaspeed after Nvidia scandal

#147. Investors led by U.S. billionaire Todd Boehly to buy Lukoil's oil and gas assets outside Russia, sources say

Investors led by U.S. billionaire Todd Boehly's asset management company Eldridge have struck a deal to buy Lukoil's assets outside of Russia, sources confirmed to CNBC.

The U.S. International Development Finance Corporation and Catena Investments, based in the United Arab Emirates, are also parties to the deal, the sources said.

The deal transfers Lukoil's international refineries and other production assets in Bulgaria, Romania, Africa and the Middle East to the U.S.-led group, the sources said. It also includes retail gas stations in the U.S., Europe and Asia, they said.

The U.S. Office of Foreign Assets Control is expected to sign off on the deal but final approval is still needed, the sources said. The deal would give the U.S.-led group about 350,000 barrels per day of production.

The agreement would allow Europe to buy fuel such as diesel previously produced by Lukoil through the U.S.-led group rather than Russia.

#145. Zoe Kleinman: Nobody is irreplaceable - unless you're Jensen Huang

Remember the tech boss and his head of HR, both married to other people, who were caught on camera at a Coldplay concert with their arms wrapped around each other?

Their horrified reaction went viral, and the pair both resigned. Astronomer, the firm where they both worked, said their actions had fallen short of its standards.

Compared with the scandal which has engulfed Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang in recent weeks, that Coldplay kiss cam moment now feels rather tame.

Huang admitted to having had an affair, reportedly with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy.

There were suggestions that she may have been involved in smuggling Nvidia chips into China, in breach of a US export ban.

Concerns about national security had reached the desk of US President Donald Trump.

There were other stories about some of Huang's relationships with female colleagues.

For most of us, that would without question amount to instant, and irreversible, dismissal.

But while Huang briefly left the company, he was just as swiftly reinstated, and welcomed back with open arms.

As I watched footage of him being greeted with cheers, applause, high fives and a big hug back at the company's HQ on Monday, I wondered how many disgraced bosses would kill to be in his shoes.

"Imagine being that Coldplay CEO reading all the comments on here about how it's OK if you're actually a good enough CEO," quipped menswear writer Derek Guy on X, external.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

Huang has blown out of the water the old adage that nobody is irreplaceable.

Despite names of potential permanent replacements being bandied around, Nvidia's board evidently reached the same conclusion - interim chief executive Colette Kress was in for less than two weeks.

Well firstly, Huang is the boss of a company that has manoeuvred itself into a uniquely powerful position.

Nvidia's computer chips are considered the best in the business for driving AI.

Demand for its latest tech is through the roof and the firm sets its prices accordingly: Its Rubin and Vera GPUs sell for tens of thousands of dollars each, and one data centre might contain hundreds of them.

So, the US AI sector relies on Nvidia, Trump is determined that the American AI industry needs to be the best in the world, and Huang is the only leader the company has ever known.

He's been at the helm for 33 years and he's a computer scientist himself, renowned for being very hands-on with the development process. (Whether the boss's input is always wanted is another matter, but it's always there.)

Long hours are normal and negative feedback is often given in front of everyone else.

When he wants something to happen, he doesn't stop until it does: one of his early ideas was to halve the chip production cycle, in order to get one step ahead of the competition with their longer lead times for orders.

Lots of experts told him it couldn't be done. Until eventually, they did it.

Not only does Huang have technical expertise, he's also a charismatic figurehead, representing Nvidia at events around the world in his trademark leather jacket, rubbing shoulders with world leaders, royalty, and fellow tech bosses.

For many, he's become something of a cult figure. Nvidia sometimes sells limited edition clothing at conference pop up stalls. A jumper featuring a cartoon image of Huang costs $178 (Â£137).

Nvidia's clothing lines quickly sell out, bought by fans of the tech giant

In addition, Huang has powerful allies, and they are mostly powerful men. US President Donald Trump personally pushed for his return to Nvidia, while Elon Musk said it would be "an honour" to work with him at SpaceX.

He is one of very few people who knows both Trump and China's President Xi Jinping personally - and somehow he has surfed the geopolitical tension between the two continents.

He has said he thinks both China and the US can win the AI race and called on them to pull together.

And finally there's the bottom line, which is always money.

Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

Huang's brief departure from Nvidia caused economic turmoil. The firm's value sank by a whopping $1tn before it climbed back up again.

It affected not only pension funds and investors around the world, but also Nvidia's own staff.

Tech employees often get to own valuable stocks in the company they work for as part of their pay packages.

Nvidia employees have been sitting on a goldmine. It wasn't really in their interests to see that drain away because of the boss's love life.

With a firm grip on the company he co-founded, cult-like status in tech circles and friends in high places, Jensen Huang created a situation where even national security concerns over the US's biggest rival couldn't topple him.

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

#143. What will the energy cap changes mean for my bills?

Energy bills are to soar in January, as prices governed by regulator Ofgem's cap rise at the coldest time of the year.

The cap affects millions of people in England, Scotland and Wales, and reflects higher wholesale gas costs which have been pushed up by the US-Israel war with Iran.

It means people on variable tariffs should give an accurate meter reading, ensure they are receiving all the support to which they are entitled, and take care over energy use.

The energy cap fixes the maximum amount which customers on standard variable tariffs can be charged for each unit of gas and electricity used.

It covers more than 20 million households in England, Wales and Scotland and is set by the regulator Ofgem every three months, external.

It affects those paying by direct debit, by standard credit (they pay when they get a bill), or prepayment meters in slightly different ways.

Most of the increase in the energy price cap has, and will be, driven by higher gas prices.

The government's decision to temporarily remove VAT from domestic electricity bills has cut about Â£45 a year from a typical bill.

Meanwhile, millions of households will receive a Â£100 automatic payment to help cover winter costs.

Customers' actual bills depend on the amount of energy they use and how they pay for it.

From January, for those on variable tariffs:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas

The roughly 11 million households on fixed tariffs will not see their prices change until the end of their fixed term.

Ofgem regulates the energy market in England, Scotland and Wales. Northern Ireland has a separate system.

As well as how much energy you use and how you pay for it, where you live, the type of property you have, how energy efficient it is, how many people live there, and the weather all make a difference.

The Ofgem cap is illustrated using "typical" household energy use in a year with a single bill for gas and electricity settled by direct debit.

The vast majority of people pay their bill this way to help spread payments across the year.

In July, Ofgem reduced what it believes to be a "typical" level of energy use, because many homes have cut back due to high prices in recent years while energy efficiency has improved.

Its new estimate is 9,500 kWh of gas and 2,500 kWh of electricity a year.

Submitting a meter reading when the cap changes means you are not charged for estimated usage at the wrong rate.

This is especially important when prices go up.

Customers with working smart meters do not need to submit a reading as their bill is calculated automatically.

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Ofgem also controls standing charges, which are a fixed daily fee to cover the costs of connecting households to gas and electricity supplies. These vary slightly by region, external and payment method.

Between 1 October and 31 December 2026, average standing charges for direct debit customers are 54.83p a day for electricity and 29.68p a day for gas.

From January, daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The electricity standing charge is lower than the amount charged over the previous three months, while the gas charge is largely unchanged.

Campaigners have long argued that standing charges are unfair because they make up a bigger proportion of the bill for low-energy households. Others say it just shifts the cost from one part of the bill to another rather than cutting it.

The government has announced plans to remove gas standing charges, although no details have been published on how that will happen

Fixed deals provide certainty for a set period â often a year, or longer â but if energy prices drop while you are on the deal, you could be stuck at a higher price.

You may also have to pay a penalty to leave a fixed deal early if you change your mind.

Experts recommend checking whole-of-market energy price comparison sites to help find the best deal and studying the details of exit fees.

Since 1 April, energy bill charges related to the low-income household insulation scheme - called the Energy Company Obligation - have been scrapped, and for three years, renewable energy projects will be 75%-funded by general taxation instead of a levy on energy bills.

Nearly everyone in England, Wales and Scotland will benefit from this cut, although the amounts will vary between households.

However, the cost of maintaining and strengthening energy network infrastructure like power lines, cables and gas pipes is rising.

In December 2025, Ofgem approved a Â£28bn investment to improve the electricity and gas grids in Great Britain to strengthen energy supplies and protect customers against volatile energy prices. It will also reduce Britain's dependence on gas.

Customers will pay part of the cost of the upgrade, through an additional Â£108 added to energy bills by 2031.

These charges started to appear from April 2026, adding about Â£6 a month to the bill for a typical household covered by the energy cap.

High electricity bills targeted in planned shakeup to energy pricing

Households could get free electricity for doing washing on sunny weekends

Suppliers must offer customers affordable payment plans or repayment holidays if they are struggling to pay their bills. Most also offer hardship grants.

Since the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022, bills have gone up by about 70% compared with the pre-crisis norm, and since then unpaid bills and charges have shot up.

Vanessa Northam, director at debt charity StepChange, has advice for people who may be struggling:

Take stock of all household money coming in and going out.

Look closely at how much energy you are using, and be alert to estimated bills, which might prove to be inaccurate.

Tell your supplier if you think you are going to struggle.

It is often best to try to talk to someone who can fully understand your situation.

Energy UK has a list of support schemes., external

Heating oil is not covered by the energy cap. About 1.5 million UK households use heating oil and they have already seen a sharp increase in bills since the Middle East conflict triggered a jump in oil prices. Some users have seen costs more than double.

The issue is particularly acute in Northern Ireland, where about 500,000 homes use heating all, almost two-thirds of all households.

In March, the government announced a Â£53m support package to help low-income households in rural communities who use heating oil.

Starmer announces Â£53m support to help with heating oil costs

How are rising costs caused by the Iran war affecting your quality of life or business?

#141. Liesman: Nvidia whiplash tests Kevin Warsh’s push to read market signals

The extreme market gyrations and the drama surrounding Nvidia this past week have highlighted the dilemma facing Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh.

On Friday, as stocks sold off, market-based probabilities that the Fed would raise interest rates declined. On Monday, with Jensen Huang back in office, a powerful rally restored market confidence that two more rate hikes are coming.

On one level, ignoring daily market vicissitudes and focusing on the long term is at the heart of one of the key changes Warsh wants to push through at the Fed. On another, he has been explicit that markets do a better job of forecasting and he wants to use market indicators more than forecasts to guide policy.

But those indicators can be subject to extreme volatility.

Take the benchmark Goldman Sachs Financial Conditions Index. Last week, the market decline and widening of credit spreads had sent a signal for the Fed to ease back on at least some of its plans to tighten policy. Some Fed commentators called for rate hikes to be taken off the table entirely. On Monday, the index eased by 22 basis points, reversing nearly half of the prior week's tightening, and both December and January hikes were back on the table.

Warsh's message, in words and deeds, is for markets to look beyond that volatility. He traveled to San Francisco last week and patiently presided for three hours over a conference on the outlook for artificial intelligence and its potential economic impact, peppering economists and tech leaders with questions about the dangers and potential upside of AI for the economy. The meeting was part of the AI task force he has convened that, in turn, is one of five to study different areas of Fed policymaking.

In his opening remarks, he said, "These groups are a way of broadening our thinking, of inviting robust discussions that take us far deeper... than fleeting data points [or] daily market moves. Our task forces are an intellectual investment to help better equip us for the next policy conjuncture and beyond."

Compare that desire for intellectual investment to Warsh's press conferences after Fed meetings. He has cut them down to a half hour, and he routinely spends much of the time parrying reporter questions about what the next Fed move will be, part of his general aversion to forward guidance. At the June press conference, he paraphrased former Secretary of State George Schultz saying, "Press conferences are useful. But when you have one, you want to make sure you have something important to say." In a regime where Warsh does not want to provide forward guidance, there is far less to say than there once was.

Here's what is known of what Warsh seems to believe about monetary policy:

In a sense, it seems, Warsh wants to make monetary policy the way he did his old job at the Duquesne Family office working for legendary investor Stan Druckenmiller. There, he evaluated startup companies, many of them with the wisp of an idea in the mind of a tech geek, for the possibility of profit many years down the road. Marked to market, those investments would show big negative numbers on the day's profit and loss statement. Down the road, some would go bankrupt and others would offer handsome returns.

Warsh's focus on AI highlights his dilemma in wanting to do policy in a different, more forward-looking and market-based way. The outlook for AI, as the chairman himself has admitted, is moving rapidly and is deeply uncertain. Markets may already be rethinking the potential earnings upside and the amount that will be spent on the AI buildout. The adoption rate of the technology is uncertain, and so are the economic effects on inflation and growth.

Warsh in San Francisco showed a particular interest in the potential for a "China Shock" to hit jobs in the U.S. service sector, a reference to how Chinese imports devastated the U.S. manufacturing sector earlier this century. If that happened, widespread unemployment could lead the Fed to lower rates over time, while the frenzied AI buildout looks now to be sparking inflation and pressuring rates upward. So there are different messages for the Fed chairman being sent from the same economic development.

It is established in the monetary policy literature that the current rate level affects the economy with what are known as "long and variable lags." Those lags mean that where policy is set today will influence the economy sometime next year. The objective is to set policy for where one thinks the economy will be next year.

Wide error bands surround most economic forecasts, including those from the Fed. But there are similarly wide error bands on market pricing. The question is, which market signals are worth watching, and for how long do they need to persist to guide policy? The Nvidia situation shows markets can be very short-term about the long-term signals they send.

#139. Pizza chains are under pressure. GLP-1 use is only one part of the problem

Big pizza chains are struggling as weaker consumer spending, a wider variety of dining options and the rise of GLP-1 medications weigh on their sales.

The pandemic was a boom time for Domino's Pizza, Papa Johns, Little Caesars and Pizza Hut. But since dining rooms reopened, the biggest U.S. pizza chains have faced an uphill battle to retain their customers.

These days, consumers are watching their discretionary spending but have more delivery and takeout options when they do decide to order, thanks to the accessibility of third-party platforms. Plus, food quality and the customer experience have become more important to diners who are looking for value when they order from restaurants.

As a result of pizza fatigue and challenges facing the broader restaurant industry, the number of U.S. pizza restaurants has tumbled. Since pizza locations hit a peak of 83,035 nationwide in August 2019, more than 9,600 pizzerias had closed their doors as of July 2026, according to data from Datassential.

In 2025, sales at U.S. pizza restaurants fell 0.3%, according to Technomic data. Sales at independent pizzerias slipped 0.4%. Chains, which account for about two-thirds of the domestic pizza category sales, saw revenue shrink 0.3%.

Papa Johns reported that North American same-store sales declined 2.5% in 2025. Pizza Hut's U.S. same-store sales fell 5%. Among the three pizza chains that were publicly traded last year, only Domino's posted annual same-store sales growth at its U.S. restaurants, with an increase of 3%.

It isn't just diners who have lost their appetite for pizza. Since March 2020, Papa Johns stock has lost about two-thirds of its value. Even shares of Domino's, which dominates the category, have seen gains of only about 2% in that time. For comparison, the S&P 500 has climbed about 150% over the same period.

And Yum Brands sold Pizza Hut this year, offloading the laggard of its portfolio to LongRange Capital and Yum China after about two decades of stagnating sales.

"Pizza Hut has been a detractor for YUM ... Going forward, YUM won't be tripped up by pizza category dynamics (discounting, etc.), translating to stronger growth and higher concentration in higher growth industry segments and markets," Wells Fargo analyst Zachary Fadem wrote in a research note in September.

Pizza chains now have significantly more competition for diners' dollars than they did in 2020. Third-party delivery platforms, which grew in popularity during the pandemic, offer a wide variety of cuisines beyond the traditional pizza and Chinese. As a result, Domino's and Papa John's have to compete with other fast-food chains, like McDonald's and Chick-fil-A, for DoorDash and Uber Eats orders.

As pizza has grown more expensive, a pie seems less appealing to budget-conscious diners. The average large cheese pizza is now almost $17, according to the 2026 Pizza Power Report from PMQ Pizza, a trade publication.

Plus, frozen pizza is another convenient option, with a lower price tag. Retail sales of single- and multi-serve frozen pizza rose 2.9% in the 52 weeks ended Nov. 7 compared with the same period in 2022, according to NielsenIQ data.

"When the American consumer is focused on the least expensive eating occasion, the items that are in my pantry, my refrigerator and my freezer win every single time," said David Portalatin, a food and food service industry advisor for Circana.

Convenience stores like Casey's General Stores have also won over customers with pizza at a low price point.

"We believe that [Casey's] has an advantage over [quick-service restaurants] and dollar stores, which have fewer options to navigate the persistent inflationary environment, while [Casey's] can offset food pressures with margins," Bank of America Securities analyst Sara Senatore wrote in a research note published in June.

She added that geographic overlap of locations suggests that Casey's is stealing share from Pizza Hut.

The biggest pizza chains have also been slow to adapt to shifting consumer attitudes toward nutrition. For example, of the four biggest chains, Papa Johns is the only one to even test a pizza crust with added protein as more diners prioritize it in their diets. And the test was only in one Atlanta restaurant — for a single day.

Pizza's nutritional content more broadly is another drag for the category. Its carbohydrate and fat content, from its dough and cheese, respectively, can make it less appealing for consumers on GLP-1 drugs, which have skyrocketed in use over the last several years.

Spending on pizza quick-service restaurants has fallen 22.2% per GLP-1 household after six to eight months using the medications, according to a 2026 survey published by PwC.

To be sure, pizza's recent struggles do not mean an existential crisis for the category. Total pizza servings are down only about 1% this year, Circana's Portalatin said.

And chains are trying to reverse that downturn in servings. In August, Domino's launched the Domino, a customizable Detroit-style personal pizza to attract solo diners. In November, Papa Johns unveiled a new turnaround plan focusing on improving food consistency and restaurant operations. And Pizza Hut is running a holiday-themed meal deal.

"Pizza's not going anywhere," Portalatin said. "The American consumer loves pizza."

#138. 'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Domestic energy prices will soar by 26% in January under the regulator Ofgem's new price cap, pushing up bills for millions of households across England, Scotland and Wales.

With household budgets already under pressure, finding ways to reduce gas and electricity costs has become increasingly important. But how much difference can simple changes really make?

The BBC spoke to three people about what they're doing to keep their energy costs down.

Kelly Anne Harvey uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms to save on the central heating

Like many people, Kelly Anne Harvey, a building surveyor from Cleveleys near Blackpool, is worried about her energy bill going up next year.

The 44-year-old, who is married with a five-year-old son, has a combined household income of Â£60,000.

She currently pays about Â£154 a month for gas and electricity on a standard variable rate with Octopus, but is looking for a fixed-rate deal to offset the rise.

The family fell into arrears on their energy bills a few years ago and Kelly says she won't let it happen again.

To control her costs she pays by direct debit, which spreads her payments and is typically Â£138 cheaper a year than paying on receipt of a bill.

The family uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms so they don't have to turn the central heating on as often.

They also take part in Octopus's energy saving scheme, which rewards customers who use less electricity at peak times, such as early evenings, with a free hour of energy at the weekend.

"On Sunday we'll turn on the dishwasher, tumble drier, washing machine and maybe even charge our electric cars," says Kelly.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, says dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

The office worker, whose daughters are aged 10 and 12, is on a fixed-term tracker tariff with E.On Next, which charges rates set below the quarterly Ofgem price cap. She expects to pay Â£1,800 for energy this year, up Â£400 on last year.

Stacey, who is part of the Changing Realities project at Glasgow University, which documents life for people on low incomes, has tried a number of things to control her energy costs.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house. The family is also using less hot water.

"I've got girls and they're growing up and they want to spend loads of time in the shower [but] it's just not possible."

Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Three reasons why your energy bill is so high

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Around 1.5 million households in the UK rely on heating oil for heating and hot water, and their costs have already soared in the past year due to disruption in global oil markets.

"When I first moved here two years ago you could get a litre of heating oil for 50-60p," says Bradley Knowles, 32, who lives in a barn conversion with his husband, mother and sister in rural Lincolnshire.

"At the height of the Iran war it went up to Â£1.20 or Â£1.30 and now it's stabilised at around Â£1."

To cut costs, the finance worker orders oil in bulk, buying 1,000-1,500 litres, preferably in the summer months when prices are lower. He also tries to order a few weeks in advance to avoid expensive next-day deliveries.

Bradley uses comparison sites to see which local suppliers offer the cheapest oil and puts money aside each month so he can always afford his orders.

Oil-powered boilers may take time to heat up, but Bradley advises against leaving them on all day as that can be very expensive.

Instead, he uses an electric tank to store his warm water and tops up the heating in parts of the property with electric radiators.

Additional reporting by Kris Bramwell & Bernadette McCague.

#134. Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Keeping yourself warm by wearing thick clothes, using blankets and having hot drinks can help reduce heating bills

Energy bills will soar by 26% in the new year as the war in Iran pushes up wholesale prices.

Typical household bills will rise by Â£37 per month as Ofgem's latest price cap comes into force from January to March, but experts say it is possible to keep rising bills in check.

Here are four areas to consider ahead of the increase.

Ofgem's price cap, as well as suppliers' fixed deals, set a limit on the price of each unit of energy, not the total bill.

So, you pay for what you use. Millions of people have cut back on their energy consumption during years of relatively high prices.

With the unit price of gas, in particular, set to soar, experts say you should take a fresh look at your energy use and renew good habits.

turning down the flow temperature on a combi boiler - usually indicated by a radiator icon, to make it operate more efficiently

turning off the pre-heat facility on some combi boilers, which would have kept some water heated up when not in use

turning down the thermostat one degree, although 18C is the recommended minimum

lowering radiator valves outside of the living room

These are small changes but - alongside washing clothes at 30C, blocking draughts and batch cooking - they can reduce energy use and bills.

Ofgem says about five million people still pay their bill when it arrives every three months, rather than receive a monthly bill often initially based on an estimate.

Taking regular meter readings, or having a smart meter, while paying by monthly direct debit solves that problem.

Paying by direct debit costs significantly less than by so-called standard credit - typically Â£138 cheaper a year.

Customers on prepayment schemes - usually people on low incomes struggling to pay bills - used to pay a higher rate than others, but now they pay slightly less than the capped direct debit variable rate.

Various deals may rely on paying by direct debit or having a smart meter.

Moving to a fixed energy tariff means the price you are charged per unit of gas or electricity does not change every three months, although the total bill will still vary depending on how much energy you use.

The trouble is that it is very difficult to work out whether a fixed deal is a good deal at the moment, for those renewing fixed deals or looking for one for the first time.

Some fixed deals are available at unit prices below the next forecast price cap level. But should energy prices fall sharply then a longer-term fix might not seem such good value compared with the price cap.

So consumer group Which? also recommends paying close attention to any exit fees when picking a fixed deal. If variable prices fall below your fixed rate and you want to get out of it before the end of your agreement period, you may have to pay a hefty sum.

Before you fix, consumer website MoneySavingExpert advises you look at all the deals available, not just what your own supplier is offering.

The government will give a Â£100 payment to millions of homes to help cover winter energy costs. It will go to homes in council tax bands A to D in England, with the equivalent expected in Scotland and Wales.

There are also a range of other forms of support specifically for people on low incomes.

This winter anyone on means-tested benefits will get Â£150 off their electricity bill.

Your supplier will apply the discount automatically unless you are in Scotland, where you will need to contact your energy company to ask for the help.

Some people will also be eligible for a Cold Weather Payment, external and most pensioners will receive the winter fuel payment.

Beyond that, most energy suppliers offer hardship grants for people struggling to pay their bills and the government's Fuel Direct Scheme, external can help you repay energy debt.

You can get more advice from Ofgem, external, Citizens Advice, external, or your own energy supplier. Trade body Energy UK has a list of support schemes, external.

People on low incomes may also be eligible for grants to help with insulating their homes, and there are local schemes offering grants for solar panels, heat pumps and the like.

But if you are hunting around for grants, watch out for scams pretending to be official money-off schemes.

How is the cost of energy affecting your quality of life? What questions do you have?
