#131. Trump's accounts traded Nvidia shares 44 times this year, records show

Donald Trump's investment portfolios have bought or sold shares in chipmaker Nvidia at least 44 times this year, a review of the US president's financial disclosures shows.

Trump's most recent public declarations of his investment trading show that between January and September the portfolios purchased between $5.2m and $17m (Â£4m and Â£13m) in Nvidia stock - and sold between $4.4m and $12m. His portfolios held between $12.5m and $58m of Nvidia shares at the end of 2025.

Financial disclosures to the Office of Government Ethics report a low and high range of transaction values, so a more precise analysis of how much Nvidia stock was traded is not possible.

The period of recent turmoil at Nvidia and volatility in its share price is not covered by the disclosures. In the last two weeks, co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang resigned and then Trump played a significant part in his reinstatement.

The White House has said that Trump's stock and bond portfolio is independently managed by third parties and neither he nor his family "has any ability to direct, influence or provide input regarding how the portfolio is invested or when investments are bought or sold".

Huang resigned on 12 November after reports of extramarital affairs, including with one woman who has alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Nvidia's share price dived following news of his resignation.

But on Monday Nvidia's board announced the 63-year-old was being reinstated as CEO.

Trump told US media he had "made the deal" to "convince" the tech giant's board to bring Huang back. The board said it had "received input from senior levels of the United States government" that he posed "no national security risk".

Nvidia's share price surged 31% on Monday following Huang's return. About $1tn (Â£763bn) was added to Nvidia's market value, but the company's shares are still trading below their value before reports of Huang's alleged affairs began to circulate.

However, concerns remain among both Republican and Democratic politicians about Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US controls over the export of advanced computer chips to China, as well as Trump's involvement in getting him reappointed as CEO.

Nvidia's chip technology and investments have been critical to the growth of the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, leading to it becoming one of the world's most valuable companies.

The firm now represents more than 6% of the S&P 500 share index, according to State Street financial services. This makes it the second most valuable company on the index after Apple, whose shares Trump's accounts also frequently trade.

Nvidia also ranks highly in many global indices, meaning that investment accounts held around the world, including pensions, are likely to include its shares.

Our analysis of Trump's Nvidia trades does not include other investment products held by him which may include its shares.

Trump made at least $2.2bn (Â£1.7bn) from a variety of investments and commercial activities, according to the financial disclosure report for the first year of Trump's second term.

Barbara Perry, a presidential historian at the University of Virginia's Miller Center, told the BBC there is "no precedent for this," adding: "It's beyond anything we've ever seen in the presidency."

#128. Trump’s meddling in Huang scandal reignites voter angst over tech’s outsized political power

When voters headed to the polls and flipped Congress this month, they sent a clear message to Washington: They wanted change.

Voters wanted curbs on the technology industry's outsized power in Washington and CEOs cozying up to President Donald Trump to sway politics in their favor.

The Monday reinstatement of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang at Trump's urging may be exactly the opposite of what voters voiced with their ballots.

"This whole ordeal only reinforces what Americans already believe about a broken political system that stands up for Big Tech CEOs and not the American people," Sacha Haworth, founder and CEO of The Tech Oversight Project, told CNBC.

Republicans are warning that Trump's unwavering support for the embattled artificial intelligence chip kingpin could further alienate voters.

"The data were so clear in the election that people don't trust big tech. The corruption message, I think, resonated a lot for Democrats in the election, and I think this sort of thing isn't helpful to Republicans in Congress who are going to be running again in two years," said Brendan Steinhauser, a GOP strategist and CEO of The Alliance for Secure AI Action, who had warned that AI data centers could be a "sleeper issue" in the lead-up to the 2026 midterms.

Before and after Election Day, voters were clear about their concern, which centered on data centers.

A Pew Research Center survey published in October found that 44% of voters considered AI very important to their vote. An NBC News exit poll found that 56% of voters said AI and data centers were crucial or a major factor in their vote for Congress. And in states including Texas, data center opposition forced GOP lawmakers who once welcomed development to change their positions.

There's a lot for tech CEOs to cheer under Trump, who was welcomed with open arms following a period of tough enforcement under President Joe Biden, and many reasons to align with the administration, said Sarah Kreps, a political scientist and professor at Cornell University who studies the intersection of technology and national security.

Chief among the reasons for celebration are the market's AI-driven gains since Trump's 2025 inauguration and his light-touch approach to tech regulation. The Nasdaq has gained nearly 21% from Jan. 17, 2025, the last trading day before the inauguration, and Tuesday's close.

"In the end, this looks like a calculated risk that the people and interests Trump prioritizes benefit more from a stable, rising market than the administration loses from the optics," Kreps said of Huang's reinstatement, adding that the Republican Party will be left to "bear the consequences" in 2028.

Outside the rarefied air of Silicon Valley and the White House, there is scant support for Huang's comeback after a series of extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy that critics say could have jeopardized national security and undermined the U.S.'s advantage in the AI race.

Trump sought to allay those fears this week when he told CNBC that he assured the board that Huang posed no national security threat. Instead, Trump used concerns over the CEO giving his talents to China as a major rationale behind the reinstatement push.

"The bigger threat would be losing the brain power of somebody like Jensen," Trump told CNBC this week. "That would be a threat to me. That would be a far bigger threat than anything we're talking about."

Haworth said the reinstatement raises concerns over how Huang's access to Trump benefits the company's business interests, especially as the U.S. investigates chip smuggling into China.

At the same time, Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick's perceived slowness to clamp down on illegal advanced AI chip sales to China, as well as his family's financial ties to Nvidia via Cantor Fitzgerald, the financial firm his sons now run, have drawn further congressional scrutiny.

"Voters are struggling with the high cost of living. They're angry about the corruption they see and a system that has been working more and more for the wealthy and well-connected, and making it harder and harder on working families," Rep. Suzan DelBene, D-Wash., chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee told CNBC.

"Corruption is an affordability issue. When they see money and favors going in places that don't help support working families, that's an affordability issue for them," DelBene said.

As Republicans try to rebuild after being routed in the midterms, the kind of leniency Trump has shown to Huang could backfire, GOP strategists told CNBC.

"Voters are tired of 'rules for thee, but not for me.' Most Americans have to live with the consequences of their decisions," said Brittany Martinez, executive director of Principles First, a center-right grassroots advocacy organization. "If you're a billionaire with the president's ear, apparently the White House will help you get your job back. That's exactly the kind of double standard that makes people wonder whose side this administration is on."

Marc Short, former chief of staff to Vice President Mike Pence and chair of Advancing American Freedom, the conservative think tank Pence founded, told CNBC that the Trump administration's involvement in Huang's reinstatement "sends a very terrible signal."

"I think it's consistent with the administration taking ownership in 41 separate private companies, to now feel that they can determine to the board who should be the CEO of one of America's biggest companies," he said.

#127. Why tiny cars are taking off across Europe

Selena Buse, 16, reverses her CitroÃ«n Ami out of a parking space and heads off past the school playground. Heads turn. The Ami looks pretty remarkable. Futurist or like-a-toaster depending on your point of view. But it's changed her life.

"I got it because I really needed it to get to school. And I use it to visit friends on the weekends. It's indispensable in rural areas like where I live," she says.

In France, you can drive a car like this from the age of 14 with a road safety certificate and eight hours training. In Italy too.

In Germany and Spain it's 15, in the UK 16, external. Because they are classed as motorised quadri-cycles. Four-wheel mopeds in other words.

Registrations have risen from about 13,000 in 2019 to around 25,000 last year in France.

In Europe, sales have gone from 30,000 to 60,000 in the past five years. And the Ami, launched in 2020, has really changed the image, with teenagers who now account for a quarter of all mini-car sales.

In Selena's very small CitroÃ«n, we are soon rolling along at the top speed of 45km/h (28mph). For other road users, no-licence cars like this can seem like mobile traffic calming.

"When I'm on faster roads and there's a lot of traffic the cars behind me sometimes honk their horns but I can't go any quicker!" Selena says.

Daniel LaprÃ¨s says the Aixam car was "the exact solution to our problem"

Another local Daniel LaprÃ¨s, who drives a micro-car made by French firm and market-leader Aixam, tells me he actually pulls over to let the cars behind pass.

He and his wife moved from Paris to this country village two years ago. Of Canadian nationality, he didn't swap his foreign licence for a French one in time and lost the right to drive. That didn't matter much when he was in Paris. Like many Parisians he didn't need a car. He used public transport. But that's not possible here.

"We tried living off of taxis and it was in the end a disaster. Then we heard about how you could drive without having to have a licence, and it was the exact solution to our problem. Cheap, inexpensive to operate, basically no administrative formalities, you get insurance."

However, LaprÃ¨s feels less safe in his small car. "They're little more than tin cans on wheels, right? I mean, you're not going to be driving fast, but someone else could be."

These cars are forbidden on motorways. But even on medium-fast roads, being considerably slower than the average vehicle can be a problem.

"You go on national highways at your risk and peril. I did it once and I will never, ever do it again. On those roads (speed limit 110km/h), everybody is passing you, including the biggest trucks. And they come at you very fast from the rear, so it's scary," LaprÃ¨s says.

Daniel is 77 so fits in with the long-established market for these cars: older people living in the countryside.

Traditionally the market also included quite a few people who'd lost their licences, often because of drink-driving offences. But the launch of the electric CitroÃ«n Ami in 2020 changed the customer profile and the product.

"The launch of the Ami was the beginning of the transformation of the sector, with more and more electric models coming on to the market. In 2026, more than 67% of new licence-free cars are electric," says Marie-Laure Nivot, market analyst at AAA Data.

The launch of the CitroÃ«n Ami in 2020 "transformed the sector" analysts say

This tendency was encouraged by stricter EU regulations on motorised two-wheelers that had been the vehicles of choice for European teenagers until then.

"It proved too costly for manufacturers to apply the new EU rules, so mopeds and small scooters have almost disappeared from the market since 2020 and licence-free cars have taken their place," Marie-Laure Nivot says.

Tiny car sales rose strongly till 2024 although they have flattened since in France with the withdrawal of subsidies for electric models.

Curiously, the arrival in this niche market of a giant competitor in the shape of Stellantis brand CitroÃ«n was actually good news for existing French manufacturers like Aixam and Ligier.

"We're specialised in this niche market and we're very good at what we do, but of course, we didn't have the communication strength of a multinational like Stellantis. They helped us to make people understand that it was possible in France to drive this product from 14-years-old, in Germany from 15-years-old. They gave a lot of visibility to our market," says Aixam sales and marketing director Tom Faget.

Aixam has invested â¬30m (Â£25m; $33m) in expanding its production capacity in France. And its sales are growing in other European countries, even as the French market slows.

France's Ligier says young people are choosing tiny cars over scooters

Meanwhile, the competition is producing increasingly sophisticated minicars - some costing as much as a small conventional car. At Ligier, marketing director Romain Illegems says the biggest change is in who's buying them.

"We've gone from mobility that was imposed on people to mobility that people choose. Before, these vehicles were something you used because you absolutely needed to get around, because there was no public transport, or because you didn't have a licence.

"Today, teenagers are choosing them instead of scooters or mopeds, because they're safer and offer better protection from the weather. Young people now account for 40% of our customers."

There are limitations, of course. Just two seats, a maximum speed of 45km/h and no motorways. But for Selena, who rents her Ami for â¬60 a month, plus insurance, it's a small price to pay for independence.

#124. Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Watch: Martin Lewis' tips on staying warm this winter

Energy bills will soar by 26% for millions of households in January after a surge in gas prices sparked by the Iran war.

A typical bill will increase by about Â£37 a month in the first three months of the new year, although the price will depend on how much energy a household uses, so it could be closer to Â£60 extra a month in winter.

A household using a typical amount of energy in England, Scotland and Wales and paying by direct debit, would see their annual bill rise from Â£1,723 to Â£2,166 if the increase was sustained for a year, said regulator Ofgem.

The government will pay millions of households Â£100 from 4 January to help cover winter costs on top of an existing VAT cut on electricity.

Charities and suppliers expect debt to rise and a wave of people to need emergency support.

Ofgem said the rise had been driven entirely by higher wholesale costs faced by suppliers owing, primarily, to the Iran war. Wholesale gas prices, paid by suppliers, were up 33% in the last three months, the period used to set the January to March cap. They have more than doubled since late 2025.

The warm home discount will take Â£150 off bills for six million households on benefits this winter. There have been calls for this support, funded through everyone's bill, to be extended.

"We are staring down the barrel of a brutal winter," said Dame Clare Moriarty, chief executive of Citizens Advice.

Neil Kenward, Ofgem's director general for markets, said it wrote to energy suppliers "reminding them of their responsibilities" to customers this winter. They have been told to identify people who need help early, make support easy to access and ensure repayment plans are affordable and sustainable.

Campaign Groups Friends of the Earth and Global Witness said major oil and energy companies are making huge profits because of spiralling oil costs, hurting households.

Energy UK, which represents suppliers, said the firms which send your bill, have profits capped on a percentage basis and do not see the same benefit as international energy generating companies. However, higher bills will mean higher profits in nominal cash terms.

The increase is the biggest since Russia's invasion of Ukraine drove up prices. It comes at a tough time of year, when the weather is at its coldest.

People on prepayment meters will be hit as their spending comes when energy is needed, rather than the cost being spread over the year as with direct debits.

The cap sets a maximum price for each unit of energy, not the total bill. Those on fixed deals are unaffected until the end of their term, but for those on variable tariffs it means:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p. The electricity price is often pegged to gas, owing to gas-fired generation.

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine.

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The cap is set every three months, based on the costs faced by suppliers as well as the cost of running the energy system.

It is based on a three-month period that ends about six weeks before the new cap comes into force. A fall in international wholesale prices in recent days will not be reflected in January's cap, but it will be in April's.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said people will be "deeply concerned" by another rise in energy bills.

"For too long, British families have been paying the price for conflicts overseas and volatile fossil fuel markets," she said.

Andrew Bowie, shadow energy secretary, said: "It is an outrage that, as bills soar, Labour are not only piling green levies and taxes onto bills but signing us up to an EU scheme that risks pushing them even higher.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, said dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house.

'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Up to 20 million households in England will get a Â£100 payment from 4 January to help with their energy bills this winter, with equivalent payments in Scotland and Wales.

Citizens Advice said the money will disappear quickly, particularly for those on prepayment meters, leading to a wave of requests for emergency help.

Energy UK said the funding was poorly targeted and insufficient to stem rising levels of customer debt.

The government said councils had a Â£100m discretionary fund to support vulnerable households which fell outside of the main scheme.

#120. US stocks fall further as fears over AI intensify

Big technology firms connected to AI continued to see share prices fall on Wednesday, with stock in Nvidia and Anthropic dropping sharply in early trading.

The surge in Nvidia shares after boss Jensen Huang was reinstated was temporary, and the general stock sell-off continued, with all three main US stock market indices down. The tech-dominated Nasdaq has shed over 2%.

The declines come amid concerns over the level and sustainability of AI investment which has rattled investors in recent weeks.

Shares in other major tech firms, including Amazon, Tesla and SpaceX, Micrsoft and Meta were also down.

Nvidia's share price was down 8% in early trading on Wednesday, extending Tuesday's slide as the boost from Huang's return eased.

The resignation and subsequent reinstatement of Nvidia boss Jensen Huang after revelations of his extra marital affairs has seen the market value of the world's premier AI chipmaker fluctuate wildly.

Given Nvidia's position as both supplier and financier of many other firms involved in the AI rollout, its rocky fortnight has had a wider impact.

The US government also announced a raft of new tariffs on countries and trading partners on Wednesday as part of President Donald Trump's flagship trade policy.

The value of big tech companies have sheltered the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, but share prices in recent weeks have been volatile in response to the US midterm elections and other events in the AI industry.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

Investors have taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms and even a moratorium on the development of new data centres.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will eventually pay off.

"It seems that these companies are trying to one-up each other on the amount of money that they're spending but you have to show that it's worth it, and that's where Wall Street is saying I don't know," said Todd Schoenberger, chief investment officer at CrossCheck Management.

Despite concerns, investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, which said the technology will remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Shares in Anthropic continued to trade below the $83 they debuted at last week, while Amazon shares fell by more than 5%.

The declines come ahead of the US stock market closing for Thanksgiving on Thursday before opening for a shortened session on Friday.

#119. US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions.

The move follows the White House's investigation into the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35% and then others facing either 20% or 25%.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of the good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing the products from abroad.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He claimed that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression" as well as "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favor while eroding America's manufacturing base".

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

"The key message is that the additional tariff burden is likely to be small, especially as various goods... are also not subject to these new tariffs."

The announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the US's reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

What tariffs has Trump introduced and why?

Trump administration pays back $100bn in 'Liberation Day' tariffs to businesses

#117. International first class is over for U.S. airlines. Enter the mega business-class cabin

The era of U.S. airlines' long-haul, international first class ended this week when American Airlines' last flight with its eight-suite section landed. In its place will be a massive business-class cabin with room for 70 passengers.

U.S. airlines have been ditching international first-class cabins for a new top tier: larger and souped-up business classes, featuring suites with sliding doors, the design du jour. Delta Air Lines and United Airlines have already taken this step. American was the last of the U.S. airlines to still have first class on long-haul international routes.

Many travelers might not notice the difference. That's because of airlines' investments into business class: new suites, bigger screens, better technology like device-charging pads, and improved bedding.

It's part of airlines' big bet on mass luxury. Consumers have been willing to shell out more to fly comfortably, which is helping to boost airline revenue. That's all the more important as costs of everything from fuel to airport landing fees have been rising, soaking up more of the industry's profits.

The retirement of first class ends a more-than-six-decade stretch of the jet age that in the early days included service like seat-side carving of chateaubriand and meals served on fine bone china.

A few decades ago, business class was much smaller, with two-by-two seats in some configurations, and recliners, not the lie-flat seats that dominate those cabins now.

"When you've got individual pods in biz class, and you've got a comfortable enough seat and bedding and decent wine, for a lot of people this is good enough," said Henry Harteveldt, founder of travel consulting firm Atmosphere Research Group and a former airline executive.

The over-the-top dining of the early days of international first class was a form of entertainment, long before laptops, personal seat-back screens and in-flight Wi-Fi, or even the promise of sleep on lie-flat beds, but customers still eat lots on board, Harteveldt said.

"People say they want to sleep but then they stay up and they eat every morsel," he said. "They're hypocrites."

There was still a hefty price gap between first and business.

A flight between Los Angeles and London on American's Boeing 777-300ER this week went for $12,994 in business class for a one-way ticket and $15,669 for a first-class seat.

American's last flight with international first class departed Friday morning local time in Sydney, bound for Los Angeles.

Its new cabin on its Boeing 777-300ERs will feature 70 business-class suites, the most of any U.S. airline. Pre-overhaul, the cabins had been operating with eight first-class suites and 52 pods in business, though the remodeling is ongoing. American is also flying a subfleet of 787-9 Dreamliners that have 51 business-class suites. (United is putting 64 new suites on that aircraft.)

Part of the allure is that some companies allow employees to book business-class tickets, but not first class.

American Airlines told CNBC it's growing its business-class cabins across the fleet. It's evaluating increasing the number of suites on its smaller long-haul jets, the Boeing 787-8 Dreamliners, from 20 currently to somewhere in the 30s.

"You don't have enough seats to make the seat cost competitive," Nat Pieper, American's chief commercial officer, said in an interview this month. "So what you need to do to make that airplane work is it's got to be a yield play. You need more business-class seats."

Differences between first and business classes on American lately came down to details.

On American's first-class long-haul section, passengers got olives with warm nuts to accompany their beverage early in the flight, while there were no olives for business class.

First class got a soup; business did not. First class got a warm dessert served a la mode in addition to the cold dessert or fruit and cheese plate both cabins were offered.

And there was no memory foam lumbar pillow for business class like there was in first, American said. But otherwise, the experiences were fairly similar for passengers. They still got better food options and service than economy passengers, along with their bigger seats, early boarding and lounge access.

"It's not like the bed can go any flatter than 180 degrees," Harteveldt said. "The bedding can be thicker, and the food nicer."

Delta and United have already replaced international, long-haul first class with dozens of their Delta One and Polaris business lie-flat suites, respectively, each of which is getting upgrades now.

American will continue offering a separate lie-flat first class on its Airbus A321T subfleet it uses for domestic cross-country flying until early 2027. But then that goes away, too.

Meanwhile, airlines over the past decade have added a premium economy section, which sits between business class and economy. It features recliner seats similar to those in domestic first class, often with foot rests, meals served on actual plates, larger seat-back screens than economy and earlier boarding.

Airlines are now cutting up business class into different segments, something they've done for more than a decade with the coach cabin's basic economy, standard and extra legroom seats.

United and Delta this year each announced their most expensive cabins will be sold with basic options, which are more restrictive and don't include things like the ability to pick your seat ahead of time or get access to a special airport lounge.

There are also a few seats in some of the business-class cabins that are roomier, like American's Flagship Suite Preferred and United's Polaris Studio. Even the guest seating in the United suite varies with "standard and companion ottomans," the latter including a seatbelt so you can ask "another Polaris passenger to visit you between takeoff and landing for a drink, chat or game," the airline says.

"I don't think we found the limit on segmentation," United CFO Mike Leskinen told CNBC on Thursday. "We have a few things on the drawing board. We're not done."

Although U.S. carriers are moving away from the designation, first class is far from dead.

Singapore Airlines, Germany's Lufthansa, British Airways and Air France have unveiled updated first-class cabins and other service improvements like food.

British Airways last month said it will build a record 106-suite business class on its Airbus A380 aircraft, the world's largest passenger airplane, but it's also upgrading its first class on board.

Meanwhile, smaller airlines like JetBlue Airways, Frontier Airlines and Allegiant Air are introducing domestic or short-haul first-class seats.

"We're going to be able to do that at a really low price comparatively to what you're used to out there in the marketplace," Frontier Chief Commercial Officer Bobby Schroeter said last month.

#115. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#114. What's gone wrong at Poundland?

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

The future of Poundland hangs in the balance after the discount retailer's collapse into administration.

Its owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, put it up for sale in September, but then rejected offers to keep the business afloat.

Poundland has a major presence on the High Street, with 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, where it trades under the Dealz brand.

Its collapse puts the jobs of its 11,000 employees at risk, as well as potentially thousands of others at firms that supply the retailer.

People have not stopped shopping at Poundland - quite the opposite.

Although the chain was struggling when it was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1, the business turned a corner over the year to September, going from a Â£68m loss to an Â£18.1m profit before interest, tax and certain accounting charges.

Going into the vital Christmas period, first-quarter like-for-like sales were up 7% and operational profits of Â£1.9m were up Â£1.2m on last year.

According to Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency specialist Begbies Traynor, "this isn't a story about shoppers abandoning Poundland".

She told the BBC the tills were "busier than they were a year ago", adding that on the face of it a turnaround seemed "well under way".

However, Gordon Brothers had been restricting Poundland's access to cash needed to restock stores and meet day-to-day expenses.

Poundland faced "a funding and ownership problem rather than a trading collapse", according to Palmer.

However, while Poundland boss Barry Williams claimed the business was making a "strong recovery", Gordon Brothers painted a different picture, saying the chain remained "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Poundland workers 'in limbo' over firm's future

It has also made about Â£18m from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

As well as being Poundland's owner, it is one of its lenders, and is owed loans, including a Â£22.5m shareholder loan it inherited when it bought the business for Â£1.

As the main creditor, Gordon Brothers would be first in line to get paid back if the business goes under - but its suppliers are not.

"When the owner is also the main lender, its first priority is recovering [loans]," says Palmer. "Sometimes the route that best protects the lender is different from the one that saves the most jobs, and that tension is why this has become such a political story."

Irish firm Martin Property Group, London-based holding company Xyra Group and German investment firm Zachert Private Equity are all also understood to have made offers for Poundland.

Gordon Brothers had also been weighing options to break Poundland up.

Its view was that ultimately breaking the retailer up would provide more security for employees in the long term, because any buyers of its store estate would also have to agree to keep the employees on.

Insiders who are critical of how Gordon Brothers has handled the Poundland sale accuse it of driving the business into the ground to make money through the insolvency process.

But the investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claim.

Now that the company has entered administration, consultants at Alix Partners have been appointed to pursue a sale of the business and its assets.

There are various options on the table, which include securing a buyer for the business either in whole or piecemeal.

The prime minister has said he wants "every effort made to secure a deal that protects jobs and keeps as many shops open as possible".

In the immediate future, Poundland stores and distribution centres will continue operating as normal.

#113. Employees at Eric Trump-backed robot startup lose health benefits as company's cash crunch deepens

Employees at Foundation Future Industries were told that their health benefits through administrator Rippling have been terminated, according to a memo obtained by CNBC, adding to mounting signs of financial distress at the humanoid robot startup advised and backed by Eric Trump.

Foundation told staff in a Friday evening email that they'd be offered coverage through COBRA, the federal law that generally lets workers temporarily keep their health insurance after losing benefits by paying the full premium out of pocket.

"Because our last pay run through Rippling was in October, they have ended our benefits with them retroactive to October," a Foundation human resources manager said in the memo. "COBRA will be offered to all employees to cover November."

Foundation has garnered headlines for the bold claims that co-founder Sankaet Pathak — whose previous startup collapsed in 2024 — has made about his robots and their potential use in war zones. Employees were last paid on Oct. 15, and some inside the company are angry at Pathak for a $4.6 million loan he took from his company last year, CNBC reported earlier this month.

A Foundation spokesman didn't immediately respond to an email or phone call seeking comment.

In the staff memo, Foundation blamed Rippling for the disruption to health coverage. The startup also said it would reimburse COBRA premiums paid by workers and that it was onboarding a new benefits provider.

But employees, especially those whose family members are undergoing treatment for medical ailments, worry the company won't have funds to reimburse workers or reinstate coverage, people with direct knowledge of the situation said.

In a statement to CNBC, Rippling indicated it was Foundation's failure to pay for its employee benefits that led to the change: "If a client enrolled in Rippling's [Professional Employer Organization] is unable to meet their payroll and benefits obligations for multiple months, they will first receive a warning and if not met, they are terminated."

Foundation has been seeking a bridge loan to repay employees after its financial woes began in August. It has been in talks with three or four venture capital firms, including existing investor Tribe Capital, Pathak has told employees, according to people familiar with the situation, who asked to remain unnamed because they were not authorized to speak publicly and feared retaliation. Tribe didn't immediately return a request for comment.

The bridge loan would help the company survive until a possible $150 million investment from South Korean conglomerate Base Group lands as early as December or January, Pathak has told employees, the people said.

Base confirmed to Reuters in recent days that it was considering an investment, but the funding has not been finalized.

The potential investment would deepen business ties between the Trump family and the South Korean conglomerate, an aluminum producer also known for real estate and hospitality projects.

Base paid the Trump Organization $2 million in 2025, according to a New York Times article, drawing scrutiny from Democratic senators over conflict-of-interest concerns. Base has also pursued other business relationships with the Trump Organization, including a Trump-branded golf course in South Korea.

Eric Trump, who has invested in Foundation and was given the title of chief strategy advisor, didn't immediately respond to a CNBC email seeking comment.

The prospect of employees having to pay for their own health coverage comes as many are already drawing down savings to cover living expenses. With another scheduled payday approaching on Nov. 30, workers are facing a third straight pay cycle without income.

Many are actively interviewing for other jobs, and attendance at the company's San Francisco headquarters has fallen sharply, according to people familiar.

Frustration has boiled over into distrust of Pathak's leadership for some employees, while others hope Pathak can secure funding to repay workers and bring the company back to financial health. In conversations and memos, Pathak says that he is highly invested in securing Foundation's future, the people said.

Despite the firm's shaky finances, Foundation continued to hire for open roles until recently and some new workers have never been fully paid, said the people.

To keep employees from leaving before a possible Base Group investment, Pathak has floated the idea of offering workers equity in the startup, the people said.

At the same time, human resources staff have warned workers against taking legal action with California to recover their wages, saying that any employees who file complaints with the state would be dismissed, the people said.

Following a Nov. 14 CNBC report detailing the missed payrolls and a $4.6 million loan Pathak took from the startup in 2025, the chief executive addressed the issue in an all-hands meeting, according to the people.

Pathak defended the arrangement by telling workers that he had plowed his own money into the startup and takes only a nominal salary, the people said.

The co-founder also told employees who were in dire financial straits to contact human resources for help covering personal bills, the people added.

"I'm burning through savings just to afford my apartment," one employee told CNBC. "It's extremely unfortunate we're in this situation."

#111. Bullish hopes spring as traders bet against a higher VIX: Options Action Live

For more reaction to Wednesday's rebound and specific trading ideas, join host Oliver Renick, CNBC contributor Mike Khouw, and guest Scott Bauer on Options Action LIVE from Cboe, by clicking the video above.With stocks mounting a comeback Wednesday, options trading across a few key volatility products suggests big swings in stocks may dampen in the near term.

More than 80,000 options traded on the Barclays S&P 500 VIX Short Term Futures ETN (VXX), almost three times the 30-day average, with more puts trading than calls, according to LiveVol data. The fund aims to track the moves in Cboe's VIX Index, which surged to 35 early Wednesday but slipped to below 28 as the S&P 500 rallied to within 25 basis points of unchanged.

At the same time, options volume on VIX itself was 50% above average, with an even more distinctive bias towards puts, suggesting traders see stocks firming, or at least, the size of the market's moves shrinking. Of the 850,000 options traded on VIX, more than 515,000 were puts, with twice as many puts likely initiated by buyers than calls, Cboe data show.

#110. Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

Poundland has collapsed into administration, putting 11,000 jobs at risk, after its owners failed to find a buyer to keep it in business.

The retailer's shops and distribution centres will remain open and continue to trade during the administration process with the business likely to be broken up and some of its stores sold off to rival retailers.

Poundland's US owners held talks with several bidders over a last-minute rescue deal, but no agreement was reached.

Poundland boss Barry Williams said in a note to staff that the board had "no alternative but to take this decision", but was making it with "sincere sadness and intense regret".

He said the US owners Gordon Brothers had removed "all borrowing facilities and access to our bank accounts" on 27 October, and adequate funds had not been released since then to keep the business solvent.

"Under these circumstances, it is impossible for the board of directors to see how Poundland can continue to operate as a going concern," he added.

Kelvin Blake, pictured at a protest in Walsall, has worked for Poundland for 25 years

Kelvin Blake, 62, who has worked for the firm for 25 years, and whose partner has been there 29 years, said he was feeling "down, depressed", and because of his age, feared he would not find a job elsewhere.

"We didn't want it, but it's come so now we've just got to face it and see what the future holds.

"My income's gone," he added. "Obviously, we'll be working until we're told to leave."

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Poundland was "part of the fabric of high streets and communities up and down the country".

He said the news of it going into administration just weeks before Christmas would be "deeply concerning for workers and their families".

He added: "While it is worrying, going into administration does give other potential buyers the opportunity to engage and we will work hard with all the relevant parties to ensure the best possible outcome."

Alix Partners, which has been appointed as administrator, said they would look at all options for the business, "including sales of the business and its assets, either in whole or part".

"We appreciate this is a deeply concerning time for all involved - customers, suppliers and, in particular, Poundland colleagues," they said.

Gordon Brothers has been criticised over how it handled a potential sale of Poundland, with accusations the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off the chain's 600 shops and stock piecemeal after running it into the ground.

Gordon Brothers, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied such claims.

On the funding question, Gordon Brothers said it had loaned the firm Â£85m over a year to help save it from insolvency.

But insiders critical of the US firm have said it stands to make its loans back and more from the insolvency process.

Previously, Gordon Brothers netted millions from liquidating the stock of fashion retailer LK Bennett, and in insolvency fees.

Labour MP Liam Byrne told the BBC the House of Commons' Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee will recall Gordon Brothers before Parliament.

He said: "I am really worried that a 'greed is good' approach to business by its owners has forced [Poundland] into an administration."

He added the committee wants to know "how much money they're ripping out and crucially, how do we make sure, as a country, that we don't have this kind of scenario unfold again".

Gordon Brothers said on Wednesday that despite the Poundland management bringing about a turnaround over the past year, the firm was "still vulnerable" to inflation and any downturn in sales or profit margin, and it had faced "several years of instability".

A spokesperson added the firm's "thoughts are with Poundland colleagues".

It said it had stepped in to take over the retailer in 2025 when it was struggling, and when "no-one else was willing to finance the business".

One of the central stumbling blocks in negotiations to keep the business afloat was a shareholder loan Gordon Brothers inherited when it bought the business for less than Â£1 last year.

Gordon Brothers was owed Â£22.5m of that loan, while the previous owner Pepco was owed Â£7.5m.

Gordon Brothers rejected various offers to buy the business as a going concern with deals to pay off that loan.

Irish firm Martin Property Group offered to pay off Â£5m of the loan up front, and defer the rest of the payments to 2030.

London-based Xyra group offered Â£12.5m up front but ran out of time to get a deal over the line.

German investment firm Zachert Private Equity is also understood to have made a late offer.

The crisis at Poundland really came to a head over the past few weeks after Gordon Brothers restricted access to funds for day-to-day operations.

This meant that stock at the retailer was not being replenished at the necessary pace in the vital run-up to the festive period.

#109. ESPN, NFL to show Super Bowl 61 in Imax theaters

Super Bowl 61 will be telecast live in hundreds of Imax theaters in February via a partnership with the NFL and Disney's ESPN, which has the broadcast rights to the 2027 game.

It marks the largest Imax live event in the company's history and the first time that the Super Bowl will be available in the format, according to a news release.

The four-hour event on Feb. 14 will include ESPN's main Super Bowl game telecast, as well as Jay-Z's halftime performance and pregame and postgame coverage.

Tickets will be available for theaters in the United States and select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil. Tickets are expected to go on sale in January. Pricing was not disclosed.

"Watching the Super Bowl with your fellow fans is one of the great shared experiences in sports, and this first-of-its-kind collaboration with the NFL and IMAX takes it to a historic new scale," said Ken Potrock, president of major event integration for The Walt Disney Co.

In addition to ESPN channels, this season's championship game will also be available on streaming platform Disney+ as well as Disney's broadcast network ABC.

#107. Saudi national in OpenAI bugging case is out on bond after DOJ tries to block release

Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an executive conference room at OpenAI's headquarters, has been released from custody, his attorney told CNBC on Wednesday.

The confirmation of Alfozan's release came a day after a federal judge in San Francisco said the defendant would be allowed out of jail on a $1 million bond, despite prosecutors' warnings that he posed a serious flight risk.

Alfozan, a former OpenAI engineer, is expected to be fitted with an ankle monitor, his attorney, David Rizk, previously told CNBC.

Alfozan was scheduled to be released from detention Wednesday morning at 8 a.m. PT. Shortly before that deadline, U.S. Attorney Craig Missakian of the Northern District of California urged the court to maintain Alfozan's detention pending trial, "given the significant risk that the defendant will flee to Saudi Arabia if he is released from custody."

In an order posted on the case docket later Wednesday, Judge Vince Chhabria said the court only learned of the government's last-minute bid to block Alfozan's release after he had already been let out.The government's motion "is therefore denied as moot," Chhabria's order stated. The judge set a hearing for Dec. 3 on the bid to revoke the magistrate judge's order releasing Alfozan.

Pointing to Alfozan's "ample resources, significant family and professional ties to Saudi Arabia, and few remaining ties to the United States," Missakian had argued in a court filing that the release conditions imposed by Magistrate Judge Alex Tse "are insufficient to safeguard the defendant's appearance for future proceedings in this case."

Alfozan "presents a serious risk of flight that no set of release conditions can reasonably mitigate," Missakian wrote.

His office sought to pause the magistrate judge's release order until a ruling on a motion to revoke it. The Department of Justice did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on Alfozan's release.

Tse in a court hearing Tuesday ruled in favor of Rizk's motion for his client's release, which said prosecutors have admitted finding "no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

In a text message to CNBC after that hearing, Rizk said, "We are simply grateful that the Court made the right decision to release Mr. Alfozan and now we look forward to defending the case."

But Missakian's filing Wednesday morning pushed back, saying "the government's investigation of this seven-week-old case is ongoing."

Prosecutors have so far found "hundreds of audio files" on Alfozan's personal computer, "including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, at least one of which involved representatives of a foreign government and many of which involved discussions of what may be trade secrets," according to the filing.

"To be sure, what the defendant did or intended to do with those audio files remains under investigation, but the absence of that evidence at this point has no bearing" on whether Alfozan is a flight risk, Missakian argued.

Alfozan was arrested Oct. 9 and is accused of concealing a recording device in the artificial intelligence company's conference room to secretly record discussions. The device was found in a USB port in a television in the room on Sept. 30, prosecutors have said.

Jury selection for Alfozan's trial is scheduled to begin Jan. 20.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#105. Some U.S. food banks see demand top pandemic levels

During the Covid-19 pandemic, Greg Silverman watched an MTA bus driver pull over on Manhattan's Upper West Side, let his passengers off and run across the street toward him.

Silverman, who runs New York nonprofit West Side Campaign Against Hunger, initially thought the driver wanted to use the bathroom at the church where the food distribution was taking place. Instead, the man asked, "Can I get some food?"

"He was a full-time employee," Silverman said. "You are an essential personnel, making the city survive and you need food."

Now, Silverman said, his organization has seen demand surpass even the levels it observed during that time of crisis when the U.S. economy ground to a halt — and many of the people now seeking food also have jobs. Silverman expects the nonprofit to distribute as much as 6 million pounds of food this year.

"The height of the pandemic was not the height of need, by any means," he said.

The pressure on the emergency food system extends beyond New York. Nationally, use of charitable food programs remains elevated as sustained inflation makes it harder for Americans to afford food, housing and healthcare. Higher prices come at the same time that the federal government has restricted food aid.

The Department of Agriculture's latest annual report found that 13.7% of U.S. households, roughly 18.3 million households, experienced food insecurity in 2024. That rate was significantly higher than in every year from 2016 through 2021.

The pandemic initially brought a surge in demand for charitable food programs, which was followed by government support. In the years that followed, pandemic-era policies such as expanded Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, or SNAP, benefits, the enhanced child tax credit and school meal programs phased out as inflation spiked.

"This is not because you can't go to work," said City Harvest President Carlos Rodriguez. "Primarily, your work and your wages are just not keeping up with the way costs have gone up in the last five years alone."

City Harvest, which works with food providers across the five boroughs of New York, told CNBC 1 in 5 New Yorkers is currently experiencing food insecurity, or limited access to enough food because of a lack of money or other resources. That is up from 1 in 6 a year earlier, and 1 in 8 before the pandemic.

Average monthly visits to food pantries and soup kitchens in New York are also 85% higher than before the pandemic, said Rodriguez.

In the Federal Reserve's October Beige Book, the central bank's New York regional branch described a sharp rise in first-time food pantry users. One nonprofit leader told the Federal Reserve Bank of New York that the strain was comparable to the pandemic, "but without the same philanthropic and government support."

Data from Plentiful, a technology platform that connects people with food pantries and tracks their usage, also points to growing demand and limited resources.

"For pantries using our reservation and pre-registration features, we see that the overwhelming majority of those are at or over capacity," said Catherine Compitello, CEO of Plentiful. "Demand is growing while supply is not."

First-time users of Plentiful in New York City rose roughly 56% from August to October, Compitello said. It was the second-largest fall surge since 2016; the only larger one came during last year's federal government shutdown, when delayed SNAP benefits drove more people to seek food assistance.

Compitello said that when families have trouble affording essentials, they often prioritize healthcare, transportation, childcare and utilities, leaving food as the expense they cut back on.

Demand for food banks is rising in other regions of the country, and nonprofits are facing their own operational challenges as they try to meet it.

A spokesperson for the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank told CNBC the organization's diesel bill more than doubled in September, to roughly $75,000, adding to the cost of delivering food and running refrigerated trucks.

Demand in the region is also spiking. Distribution in the Los Angeles area for January through September totaled 116 million pounds of food and grocery products — the equivalent of 93 million meals — and slightly higher than a near-record year in 2025, according to the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank.

Federal food aid changes have exacerbated the problems. Rodriguez of City Harvest described SNAP as a first line of defense against hunger, saying the program can provide as many as nine meals for every one meal the organization provides. But City Harvest has estimated at least 8.7% of SNAP recipients in New York City lost benefits over the past year, after congressional Republicans and President Donald Trump approved work certification requirements and changes to eligibility.

The pressure is also mounting in Los Angeles County, where roughly 260,000 CalFresh recipients are subject to expanded federal work requirements that took effect in June. CalFresh is California's version of SNAP.

That does not mean all 260,000 will lose benefits, since people who meet the work requirements or qualify for an exemption can remain eligible. But a Los Angeles Regional Food Bank spokesperson told CNBC the changes add another layer of uncertainty for households already struggling with food costs.

In Atlanta, the pressure extends beyond the city itself to the surrounding region. The Atlanta Community Food Bank serves about 270,000 households a month, or nearly 700,000 people, through partner pantries across North Georgia. President and CEO Kyle Waide said the number of people it serves has jumped more than 70% in five years.

"Much of that increase is among people whose incomes are too high to qualify for federal benefit programs," said Waide.

Under current SNAP guidelines in Georgia, most households must have gross monthly income at or below 130% of the federal poverty level to qualify. They must also meet a separate net income test. For fiscal 2027, which began Oct. 1, the gross income limit is $2,960 a month for a household of three, or about $35,520 annually, and $3,575 a month for a household of four, or $42,900 a year.

More than 60% of Atlanta Community Food Bank clients have household incomes above the cutoff for SNAP eligibility, Waide said, generally in the mid-to-upper $30,000 range depending on household size. The food bank does not collect everyone's income, he said, but the figure is based on survey estimates.

Given those dynamics, Waide said he does not expect the need to ease.

"We're preparing for the level of demand to stay where it is today, or even increase more," Waide said. "We don't see a big source of relief coming anytime soon."

#104. SpaceX wins FCC approval to deploy 1 million orbital data center satellites

The Federal Communications Commission has authorized Elon Musk's SpaceX to put 1 million artificial intelligence computing satellites in orbit, Chairman Brendan Carr announced on Wednesday.

Carr said in a post on X, the social network owned by SpaceX, that the company's Starmind constellation, and "competitor constellations will use solar power to extend America's lead" in AI.

Musk said in a reply, "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

The FCC, in its authorization filing, noted that SpaceX's application "received a substantial number of comments from the public, including petitions to deny filed by Kuiper Systems LLC (Amazon Leo), Viasat Inc. (Viasat), and the American Astronomical Society (AAS)."

In a March petition urging the FCC to deny SpaceX's application, Amazon had labeled the company's Starmind project as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan."

The e-commerce and cloud computing giant is building a competitor to SpaceX's Starlink satellite internet service, and said it would take SpaceX "centuries" to build the constellation, adding that the company provided scant details about how it would "deliver on these grand claims."

The petition resulted in a rebuke from Carr, who said Amazon "should focus on the fact" that it's falling short of its own satellite deployment milestone.

Amazon declined to comment on the FCC's approval.

Other companies have also set their sights on orbital data centers. Google last year announced Project Suncatcher, which it describes as a "moonshot to put AI in space," and launched its first chips into space on a SpaceX rocket and Planet Labs satellite in October.

In March, Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin petitioned the FCC to let it launch up to 51,600 satellites as part of a low Earth orbit satellite constellation dubbed "Project Sunrise." The constellation would work in tandem with Blue Origin's planned TeraWave satellite internet service, announced in January.

Astrophysicist and author Adam Becker was critical of the FCC move in an email to CNBC on Wednesday.

"Any serious attempt at this is going to further pollute the night sky and runs a serious risk of a cascading chain reaction of space junk collisions, which would be a disaster for all of humanity," he wrote. "Musk and Carr are either profoundly stupid themselves, or hoping the rest of us are too stupid to notice this is nonsense."

SpaceX did not respond to a request for comment.

The FCC said in its authorization that it dismissed "comments and concerns raised regarding topics over which the Commission has no authority, including environmental concerns," and concerns about its impact on "optical astronomy."

#102. Best Buy reports comparable sales growth of 6.1%, highest in five years

Best Buy on Tuesday beat Wall Street expectations on the top and bottom lines for its third fiscal quarter and raised its full-year guidance.

The consumer electronics retailer reported comparable sales growth of 6.1%, a significant beat over expectations of 2.3%, according to market intelligence platform StreetAccount. The company said that growth was its highest in five years. It reported a domestic comparable sales increase of 6.2% and international comparable sales growth of 4.9%.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

Best Buy also raised its guidance for the rest of the year. It's now expecting full-year revenue of between $43 billion and $43.3 billion compared with a previous expectation of between $42.3 billion and $42.8 billion. It's expecting comparable sales to be up 3.4% to 4% compared with prior guidance of between 1.9% and 3%.

The earnings report is the company's first since Bonfig took over as CEO from Corie Barry at the beginning of the month.

Bonfig said the retailer saw overperformance in both computing and gaming for the quarter, driven by recent game launches and a targeted product assortment.

Looking ahead into the holiday season, he said the Best Buy consumer remains "resilient," but that they are focused on getting value wherever they can.

"We did see not only our current customers continue to be engaged, but we actually did see more new customers come into the brand, as well as we had some customers that we re-engaged as well," Bonfig said. "So those are really, really positive signs for us in Q3, and all of that was factored into the way that we guided for Q4 and the rest of the year."

Here's how the company performed in the period ended Oct. 31 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Best Buy reported net income of $353 million, or $1.66 per share, compared with $140 million, or 66 cents per share, the year prior. Adjusting for one-time items, including restructuring charges, Best Buy posted adjusted earnings of $1.62 per share.

Revenue rose to $10.24 billion from $9.67 billion in the year-ago period. Best Buy reported domestic revenue of $9.42 billion and international revenue of $820 million.

Tuesday's earnings are the latest in a series of strong reports from the retailer, which has been instituting a turnaround strategy to return to growth after getting hit by declining foot traffic, lower consumer sentiment, tariffs and memory costs.

Shares of Best Buy are up nearly 45% this year.

Bonfig told CNBC last quarter that the launch of "Grand Theft Auto VI" in the current quarter is expected to drive sales in the gaming category, which has been seeing gains over the past year. Last month, Best Buy launched a new gaming experience for customers featuring gaming content, technology and special offers, among other features.

"I think there'll still be opportunities for gaming to continue to drive our business moving forward," Bonfig told CNBC on Tuesday.
